## Toward Recursive Self-Improvement in Physical AI

Meng Luo1 Zechen Bai1 Shanqing Xu2 Yanlin Li1 Kaixin Li1 Mengchen Qian2 Weifeng Lu3 Ran Zhang4 Yingtian Zou3 Ziyang Luo5 Wei Chen2 Hongzhan Lin1*

1National University of Singapore 2Huazhong University of Science and Technology 3Shanghai Jiao Tong University 4Nanyang Technological University 5Hong Kong Baptist University

\titlelogos

{physicalaifrontmatter}Recursive self-improvement (RSI) describes a system that improves not only its behavior but also the process by which it improves. Recent digital agents test this idea by rewriting their own code and checking each version with executable tests. Bringing these mechanisms into the physical world requires learning from partial observations through costly trials, while accounting for changing operating conditions and potentially irreversible consequences. We position RSI within robot learning and trace pathways from improving robot capabilities to improving the processes that generate them. We examine how physical experience can strengthen these improvement mechanisms and when the resulting gains can support further self-improvement of the mechanisms themselves. Gains carry forward only when feedback addresses remaining learning needs, the receiving component can use the change, its value survives changes in tasks or hardware, and continued practice remains safe and affordable. Existing studies, concentrated in manipulation, support behavioral improvement, benefits to later learning, and procedure improvement. Some systems also change the improver’s knowledge or tools, but the physical studies reviewed here have yet to isolate gains attributable to recursion. We synthesize these findings into evaluation principles and research directions for machines that, trial by trial, safely improve the way they improve.

## 1 Introduction

Recursive self-improvement (RSI) describes a system that improves the process by which it improves, not only its behavior. The idea goes back to I. J. Good’s speculation about machines that design better machines (Good, 1966) and to self-referential learners such as the Gödel machine (Schmidhuber, 2003), and recent digital agents have begun to approach it (Chen et al., 2026d). STOP (Zelikman et al., 2024) applies a program that improves code to its own source, and the Darwin Gödel Machine (Zhang et al., 2026c) lets improved coding agents modify themselves; both judge each candidate with executable tests. Yet a system can get better at a task, and a fixed procedure can keep producing better versions of it, without anything recursive happening. RSI requires two further links (Section 2.2): what the system retains from earlier improvements must make its next revision better, and the improver, the component that revises the learning procedure, must in turn use what it has gained to help produce its own next improvement.

Physical RSI asks the same question of systems that learn by acting in the physical world, such as robots. Digital RSI supplies the mechanisms (language models that write reward code or controllers, archives of earlier variants, and automated experiment loops); physical RSI tests how these mechanisms use physical experience and whether they hold under physical constraints (Figure 1). The physical setting changes the experimental conditions in four ways (Table 1). First, physical outcome checks depend on sensors and task-specific verifiers, people, or learned detectors whose reliability must be established; even an automated grasp-success check can miss what it does not sense, and an error in that judgment can be reinforced and then executed on hardware. Second, every candidate costs robot time, resets, supervision, and wear, so a revision must pay off within few trials. Third, practice changes its own conditions: objects move, grippers wear, calibration drifts, and rolling back software does not repair a damaged object. Fourth, what can be improved extends beyond prompts and code to controllers, dynamics models, and the interface to a particular body. Each difference bears on the central question of RSI, whether what one round of revision teaches the improver makes the next revision better.

Table 1: Experimental conditions of digital and physical self-improvement. Digital examples include STOP, AlphaEvolve, and the Darwin Gödel Machine.

Consider a robot arm that keeps missing the same grasp. The failure can have several causes, and each calls for a change kept in a different place; these places organize Sections 3.1 and 3.2. The robot’s policy, the learned controller that turns observations into motor commands, may never produce the recovery motion; an expert correction can teach it, as RECAP (Physical Intelligence et al., 2026) learns from deployment experience and corrections (Section 3.1.1). The robot may mistake a light touch for a stable hold because its model of contact is wrong; a world model refitted to contact data can correct the prediction and give the policy a place to practice, as in PlayWorld (Yin et al., 2026b) (Section 3.1.2). The robot may have recovered from this failure before without keeping what worked; external knowledge, such as a saved recovery or program, can be called the next time, as Zetta (Ding et al., 2026) grows code-based critics and recoveries around a frozen policy in simulation (Section 3.1.3). Finally, the robot may keep choosing the wrong kind of repair; then the learning procedure must change: which attempts to collect, how to judge them, and how to turn them into updates (Section 3.2). Eureka (Ma et al., 2024a) rewrites reward code from simulated training results, and ENPIRE (Xiao et al., 2026b) lets coding agents modify the training infrastructure from robot experiments. The first three routes improve what the robot does; the fourth improves how it learns, and it is where physical RSI would begin. Recursion would further require that knowledge of why earlier repairs worked, which we call repair knowledge, improves how the next procedure revision is made. Figure 2 contrasts this route with applying a fixed training recipe to new experience.

Figure 2: Improving what the robot does and how it learns. On the left, the learner improves under a fixed recipe and retains the change in its policy, world model, or external knowledge. On the right, the improver revises the learning procedure using retained repair knowledge; the dashed return asks whether that knowledge improves how the next revision is made (R1), and whether the improver, once improved, then helps produce its own next improvement (R2). The bottom row lists the physical limits behind the four conditions that let gains carry forward.

Our central argument is that physical experience can improve task competence, the learning procedure, and the improver that revises this procedure, and that four recurring conditions, each sharpened by the physical setting, decide whether such gains carry forward. Each can fail: the new signal may not address what the learner still lacks, because sensing shows only part of a failure; the receiving component may be unable to absorb it on its body; its value may not survive later changes in behavior or hardware; or further practice may become unsafe or unaffordable. For the grasping robot, this could mean collecting more of the grasps it already makes well, refining a contact model that its controller cannot use, relying on a fix that stops working once the gripper is replaced, or practicing until the gripper wears out. A change can improve execution and still fail to help later learning: added autonomous data often yield modest gains that saturate and can even regress (Mirchandani et al., 2025).

From learning actions to improving how learning happens.

Several research traditions supply pieces of this progression. Self-supervised grasping showed that physical outcomes alone can supervise behavior at scale, first by learning from tens of thousands of real grasp attempts (Pinto and Gupta, 2016) and then through large-scale real-robot reinforcement learning (RL) with QT-Opt (Kalashnikov et al., 2018). Interactive imitation learning, as in DAgger (Ross et al., 2011) and HG-DAgger (Kelly et al., 2019), showed how an expert can correct the learner in the states that the learner itself reaches. Lifelong robot learning (Thrun and Mitchell, 1995) asks how knowledge gained on earlier tasks can reduce the real-world experimentation needed for later ones; model-based RL, from Dyna (Sutton, 1990) and PILCO (Deisenroth and Rasmussen, 2011) to Dreamer (Hafner et al., 2020), asks how recorded transitions can support practice inside a learned model; and skill-based approaches, such as options (Sutton et al., 1999) and the shared RoboEarth knowledge base (Waibel et al., 2011), ask how an extended behavior can be stored and called again. Each tradition widened what one learning episode can pass on to the next.

Recent generalist and foundation-model-based systems widen both what can change and what role the change plays. RoboCat (Bousmalis et al., 2024) turns a policy adapted to a new task into a source of training data for the next generalist, and SILVR (Luo et al., 2026a) revises a video-based planner using its own executions. In RoboCat, Eureka (in simulation), and ENPIRE, experience reaches the machinery that produces future updates (a data collector, a reward function, the training infrastructure) as well as the policy that receives them. This brings the physical problem close to the long-standing aspiration of learning to learn (Thrun and Pratt, 1998), and several reviewed systems already contain the components that a demonstration of recursion would need. The open question is how to realize that aspiration with limited trials, imperfect sensing, and a body that changes over time.

Scope and related surveys.

Our scope is the improvement of policies, world models, memories, skills, and the software procedures that update or use them, whenever their evidence or evaluation is connected to physical action. We discuss hardware change when it affects compatibility, maintenance, or continued experimentation. Autonomous morphology design and physical self-reconstruction are adjacent topics outside our scope.

Recent surveys cover neighboring ground (Table 2). Some focus on agents and embodiment: recursive self-improvement in AI systems (Chen et al., 2026d), self-evolving agents (Gao et al., 2025), weights versus executable skills as stores of improvement (Jena et al., 2026), self-evolving embodied AI (Feng et al., 2026) and humanoids (Nguyen et al., 2026), and levels of physical recursive self-improvement (Zhou et al., 2026a). Others analyze world models for robot learning (Hou et al., 2026) and manipulation (Wang et al., 2026a), joint world–action models (Lu et al., 2026b), actionable simulators (Chen and Zhu, 2026), verifiers for robot policies (Wan et al., 2026), and physical AI broadly (Xiang et al., 2025). Our organizing question is different: after a change has been acquired, who uses it next, and what does that use improve? Component names alone cannot answer it, because two data collectors with similar task success can yield training data of very different value; a more accurate predictor can still produce less useful training data for a policy; and the same kind of retrieved memory can change either how a program is repaired or how an outcome is judged.

The review makes three connected contributions. First, it positions RSI within robot learning by separating method paradigms and problem settings (from reinforcement learning and meta-learning to AutoML) from a ladder of claims (behavioral improvement, later-learning benefit, procedure improvement, and the two links of recursion), each defined by the comparison that would establish it. It organizes physical self-improvement along that ladder according to where experience leaves its change and which decision of the learning procedure it revises, showing that data volume and component accuracy are unreliable proxies for progress. Second, it separates mechanisms that change an improver’s knowledge or tools from evidence that the improver has improved: existing studies support the first three claims, while recursive gains still need dedicated verification. It reviews digital precedents and physical boundary cases and specifies a two-round experiment that could isolate the recursive relation, together with its controls and an estimate of its hardware cost; Table 13 records where the reviewed studies stand on each claim. Third, it names the four breakdowns above as recurring conditions (informative feedback, usability, robustness to distribution shift, and continued practice) and shows how each fails and which capability would restore it. Four research questions organize the review.

RQ1

What limits a robot’s next improvement?
Is useful experience missing, or is progress limited by supervision, optimization, action generation, interfaces, or retention?

RQ2

Which changes address that limitation?
How does experience change behavior, reusable models or skills, and the collection, supervision, curriculum, or update procedures used in later learning?

RQ3

When can improvements build on one another?
When do improvements carry forward into better learning procedures and improvers, and what evidence would establish recursive self-improvement?

RQ4

What makes self-improvement different in the physical world?
How do sensing, embodiment, reset, safety, cost, and hardware changes constrain the process?

Section 2 lays the foundations: a primer for newcomers (Section 2.1), where improvement is kept, the ladder from self-improvement to recursion, the position of RSI relative to neighboring learning paradigms (Section 2.3), and the benchmarks and metrics used in the reviewed studies (Section 2.5); Figure 3 maps the review, and Appendix C collects the terms. Section 3 climbs the ladder, from improving the learner (Section 3.1) and the learning procedure (Section 3.2) to improving the improver (Section 3.3) and closing the recursive loop (Section 3.4). Section 4 turns to what current evaluation cannot yet measure, why gains stop compounding, how practice can stay safe, and which problems remain open, including the two-round test that recursion would require.

## 2 Foundations of Physical RSI

We follow every system in this review along one path: from experience, to a retained change, to the change’s next use. Later sections use this path to work out when a gain survives another round, without assuming a common architecture.

## 2.1 Learning to Act

A few groups of ideas are enough to follow the rest of the review. We introduce them with the running example of the robot that keeps missing a grasp.

Policies.

A policy is the controller that maps what the robot currently observes (camera images, joint angles, force readings, and possibly a language instruction) to its next motor command; for the grasping robot, it turns a view of the object into the next movement of the arm and gripper. In the systems reviewed here, policies are usually neural networks. Generalist policies are trained on large collections of robot data spanning many tasks, as in RT-1 (Brohan et al., 2023b), and sometimes many robot bodies, or embodiments, as in Octo (Octo Model Team et al., 2024). A vision-language model (VLM) reads images and text and outputs text; robot systems often use one to propose goals or to judge whether an attempt succeeded. A vision-language-action (VLA) model is a generalist policy built on a pretrained VLM, so that it reads images and an instruction and outputs actions; RT-2 (Brohan et al., 2023a), OpenVLA (Kim et al., 2024), and $\pi_{0}$ (Black et al., 2025) are examples. Many recent policies output a short sequence of future actions at once, called an action chunk.

Learning from demonstrations.

The simplest way to obtain a policy is imitation learning from demonstrations, which are often recorded while a person teleoperates the robot (Argall et al., 2009). In behavior cloning (BC), the policy is trained to reproduce the demonstrated action in each recorded situation. BC learns only from recorded situations, so its small errors can compound: if the gripper drifts slightly off the object into a state that no demonstration covers, the policy may have no good action to take. Interactive methods such as DAgger (Ross et al., 2011) address this by asking an expert what to do in the situations that the learner itself reaches. In practice, a person often takes over when the robot errs, and these interventions supply corrective training data.

Learning from rewards.

In reinforcement learning (RL) (Sutton and Barto, 2018; Kober et al., 2013), the robot instead tries actions and receives a reward that scores the outcome, for example 1 for a stable grasp and 0 otherwise. On a real robot, rewards do not arrive automatically: a person, a hand-written check, or a learned reward model, such as a success detector, must judge each attempt. We call a component that supplies such judgments for learning an evaluator. Because rewards are often sparse and delayed, many methods learn a value function, or critic, that predicts how much future reward to expect; some systems use the word critic more loosely for any component, learned or hand-written, that scores behavior. The advantage of an action measures how much better it is than what the policy would typically do in the same situation. Off-policy methods can learn from experience collected by earlier versions of the policy or by other sources, which they keep in a replay buffer. On hardware, every trial costs time, supervision, wear, and a reset that returns the scene to a usable starting state, so the source of the reward and the number of available trials become central resources.

Improving an existing policy.

Large pretrained policies are rarely retrained from scratch. They are fine-tuned, or post-trained, on new data. A small residual policy can add corrections on top of the actions of a frozen base policy, such as a slight change to the angle at which the gripper approaches the object. Distillation trains one policy to imitate another, for example to fold a specialist’s behavior back into a generalist. When a policy learns a sequence of tasks, it can forget earlier ones, a problem studied in continual learning (Parisi et al., 2019); replaying stored data from earlier tasks is a common protection.

Models of the world.

A world model, or dynamics model, predicts what will happen after an action, for instance the next camera image or the next object positions (Ha and Schmidhuber, 2018; Hafner et al., 2020). The grasping robot’s contact model is one example: it predicts whether a touch will become a stable hold. A robot can use such a model to plan, by imagining the outcomes of candidate actions, or to generate imagined experience on which a policy is trained. A physics simulator is a hand-built model of this kind; a learned world model is fitted to recorded robot data and can be wrong in ways that a policy may learn to exploit.

Improvements outside the weights.

Not every improvement is stored in network weights. A system can keep memories of earlier episodes, maintain a library of callable skills (Sutton et al., 1999), or write programs that compose perception and control functions (Liang et al., 2023). Large language models are increasingly used to write such code or to revise reward functions (Ma et al., 2024a).

Simulation and hardware.

Results come either from simulation benchmarks or from hardware experiments on physical robots, and training in simulation before deployment on hardware is called sim-to-real transfer. Section 2.4 discusses what each setting can establish.

## 2.2 Defining Recursive Self-Improvement

Where experience leaves a change.

We consider loops in which experience produces a retained change that affects later attempts or learning. Human demonstration, correction, reset, or approval can remain part of the loop: automation concerns who makes each decision, whereas learning concerns whether experience changes it (Table 17 in Appendix A). A failed update can also be informative by showing where this connection breaks.

Generic pretraining without a subsequent experience–update–use comparison serves as background, as does adaptation confined to a single attempt. Rapid Motor Adaptation (Kumar et al., 2021), for example, infers current conditions from recent history without retaining the adaptation. Zeva’s interaction memory is a boundary case: it carries across retries within an episode but not across episodes or learning rounds. Simulation-only and formal studies supply enabling mechanisms, while meta-learning, AutoML, and digital agents supply precedents (Section 2.3); direct evidence about physical RSI must come from physical systems.

A retained change can serve four uses (Figure 4). It can choose or execute actions, predict outcomes or supply practice, support reuse (for example, calling a saved recovery or consulting guidance for a new program), or change how later data are collected, judged, and turned into updates. These uses are usually carried by policies, world models, external knowledge, and learning procedures, respectively (Sections 3.1 and 3.2). They can overlap: a policy can collect training data, a model can judge outcomes, and a memory can guide an updater. Each study is discussed mainly under the use its experiment tests, regardless of whether its authors call it self-evolving or recursively self-improving.

Figure 4: Where a retained change is kept and how it is used. Each row follows the grasp example from physical experience to the retained change and its immediate use; the italic line names the use. Dashed tags mark secondary handoffs into the learning procedure.

Learner, learning procedure, and improver.

The learner acquires task capabilities; the learning procedure determines what experience to collect, how to judge it, and how to construct updates. An improver proposes or selects revisions of that procedure. It may be a person, a fixed rule, a search process, or a learned component, with knowledge in memory, weights, code, or prompts. Figure 2 separates the two levels: the learner improves under a fixed recipe, whereas the improver revises the recipe itself.

In the grasp example, adding a corrected grasp to the training data is a repair, an update made in response to an observed failure or limitation. The rule choosing which failed grasps need correction belongs to the learning procedure; whoever devises or changes that rule is the improver. The object being changed determines the role: an editor of task skills or data belongs to the procedure, whereas an editor of rewards, evaluators, collection rules, or update-generating training code acts as an improver.

From better behavior to recursion.

We distinguish four claims (Table 3), and evidence for an earlier claim does not establish a later one. Behavioral improvement means better execution under a common task test. A later-learning benefit means that retained data, weights, models, skills, or code help a subsequent learner. Procedure improvement means that a revised collector, evaluator, editor, or updater teaches better than its earlier version. This comparison requires matched learners, which differ only in the component under test and share starting conditions, resources, and evaluation protocol (Figure 8).

We operationalize recursive self-improvement through two further links, which this review uses as empirical criteria for attributing a gain to recursion. First (R1), repair knowledge retained from earlier repairs must improve the improver: on new failures, its procedures must teach matched learners better than those produced by its earlier version. Second (R2), the improver, once improved, must help produce its own next improvement: given the same new repair histories, a self-revision proposed using its acquired knowledge must yield an improver that outperforms both the improver it revised and a revision of that same improver proposed with the acquired knowledge frozen. A fixed improver can repeatedly improve procedures without satisfying either link; an external designer’s growing expertise does not establish system-level recursion either. Section 3.3 develops the grasp example. A concurrent survey grades physical RSI by whether an update changes capabilities or the improvement procedure and whether its gain holds on the same or on other tasks, reserving its top level for an independently verified recursive gain (Zhou et al., 2026a); the claims here are instead defined by the comparison that would establish each. The name is shared by other concurrent work. PhysRSI-eval (Li et al., 2026j) applies it to agents with frozen weights that revise code, tools, and notes after each graded attempt, and Physical RSI 1.0 (MMLab & PhysicalRSI contributors, 2026) names a system in which a multimodal foundation model revises the harness through which a robot acts. We read these works, like any other, by the comparisons they report; a name does not establish either link. In symbols, write $L$ for a learner, $J(L)$ for its outcome under a protected success evaluation, and $P=(c,e,d,u)$ for a learning procedure whose components choose what experience to collect, evaluate outcomes, select the training data, and apply the update rule; $P(L)$ is the learner that $P$ produces from $L$ under a fixed budget. Behavioral improvement means $J(L^{\prime})>J(L)$ . Procedures are compared from a common starting learner $L_{0}$ with matched resources and evaluation, and we write $J(P)=J(P(L_{0}))$ : procedure improvement means $J(P^{\prime})>J(P)$ , and a later-learning benefit from a retained artifact $A$ means $J(P(L_{0};A))>J(P(L_{0}))$ , where $P(L_{0};A)$ is trained with access to $A$ . An improver $I$ maps failure histories $F$ to a procedure $I(F)$ . Write $I^{K}$ for the improver with knowledge $K$ , $K_{0}$ and $K_{1}$ for its knowledge before and after earlier repairs, and $\rho_{K}(I,H)$ for the improver obtained when a self-revision of $I$ is proposed from repair histories $H$ with knowledge $K$ available. On new failures $F$ , held out from everything the improver has learned from and from $H$ , the two links require

R1:
 $\displaystyle J\bigl(I^{K_{1}}(F)\bigr)>J\bigl(I^{K_{0}}(F)\bigr),$ 

R2:
 $\displaystyle J\bigl(\rho_{K_{1}}(I^{K_{1}},H)(F)\bigr)>\max\bigl\{J\bigl(I^{K_{1}}(F)\bigr),\ J\bigl(\rho_{K_{0}}(I^{K_{1}},H)(F)\bigr)\bigr\}.$ 

In R1, the right-hand side is the frozen-knowledge control. In R2, the first term requires the successor to improve on its parent, so a revision that changes nothing cannot pass; the second revises the same parent with the acquired knowledge frozen and so isolates what that knowledge contributes to the revision. Withholding the acquired knowledge while keeping the same parent is straightforward when that knowledge resides in a memory snapshot, code, or prompts; when it is stored in the improver’s weights, the R2 control needs a design of its own.

Table 3: The comparison each claim requires. All comparisons use matched learners and budgets; in R2, the updated improver is revised once with its acquired knowledge and once with that knowledge frozen, and the successor must also beat the updated improver itself (inequalities in Section 2.2).

Claim

Question

Relevant comparison

Behavioral improvement

Does the system act better?

 $J(L^{\prime})$ vs $J(L)$ ; common task test

Later-learning benefit

Does it help a later learner?

 $J(P(L_{0};A))$ vs $J(P(L_{0}))$ ; matched learners

Procedure improvement

Does the new procedure teach better?

 $J(P^{\prime})$ vs $J(P)$ ; matched learners

Recursion, R1

Updated vs earlier improver, new failures

Recursion, R2

Does the improved improver improve itself?

Acquired vs frozen knowledge; vs parent

How far a result supports recursion.

For R1 and R2, we distinguish mechanism only, where machinery could change the improver; an uncontrolled result, consistent with a link but without a control that withholds only what was acquired; and a controlled result, which tests that contribution with matched learners and budgets (Section 4.4). The claim and its level of support are separate.

Two controls answer different questions. A frozen-knowledge control withholds acquired repair knowledge while retaining the improver, testing that knowledge’s contribution to the procedures produced (R1) or the self-revision proposed (R2). A fixed-improver control keeps the initial improver while the rest of the system improves. A gain over it shows that revising the improver contributes to later improvement, bearing on R2, but does not identify which acquired knowledge caused the revision.

What lets gains carry forward.

Four conditions recur across these claims. Informative feedback distinguishes the remaining learning need. Usability means that the receiving component can represent, optimize, and execute the change. Robustness to distribution shift asks whether the change stays useful as the policy, model, task, or hardware changes, including changes caused by improvement itself. Continued practice asks whether another informative trial remains safe and affordable. These four conditions overlap and serve as diagnostic lenses rather than independently validated constructs. Section 4.2 examines their failures, and Figure 3 connects these conditions to the uses and claims above.

## 2.3 Relating RSI to Robot Learning

Reinforcement learning and meta-learning already let robots improve from experience and learn how to learn. Self-training, continual learning, and automated machine learning (AutoML) let systems learn from their own outputs, keep learning across tasks, or search for better training recipes. It is therefore fair to ask what RSI adds. These are method paradigms or problem settings, which say how an update is computed or what it must cope with, whereas the claims of Section 2.2 say what an experiment shows to have changed. An RSI system may use any of them to revise any of its components, so the categories overlap rather than exclude one another: RSI is not a further paradigm after meta-learning but a level of improvement that a system built with any of them might reach (Table 4).

Table 4: How learning paradigms and problem settings relate to the claims of Section 2.2. The last four columns show how each approach can support each claim; the R1, R2 column concerns whether the improvement mechanism itself improves.

Self-improving search

Reinforcement learning.

Standard RL improves a policy under an objective and update mechanism chosen before training (Sutton and Barto, 2018). Run repeatedly on hardware, as in HIL-SERL (Luo et al., 2025) and RECAP (Physical Intelligence et al., 2026), it yields behavioral improvement, and its data or policy can supply a later-learning benefit. RL loops can also be nested inside an outer optimization over part of the learning procedure. Eureka searches over reward programs using the RL training results they produce (Ma et al., 2024a), and meta-gradient RL adapts the return while the agent learns (Xu et al., 2018). LPG discovers an update rule (Oh et al., 2020), and DiscoRL learns such a rule across many agents and environments and applies it to unseen benchmarks (Oh et al., 2025). These loops target procedure improvement, while the outer search itself stays fixed. DERL (Cheng et al., 2025) departs from this on text-based and mathematical reasoning tasks: it trains its reward proposer by policy gradient on the validation success of the inner policies, so the search learns from its own trials, while the rule that trains the proposer stays fixed. In the grasp example, RL that tunes the grasp under a fixed slip penalty improves behavior, whereas a search that revises the slip penalty changes the procedure; further rounds of the first kind do not become the second.

Self-training.

Many systems called self-improving train on their own outputs. In self-training such as Noisy Student (Xie et al., 2020), a teacher trained on labeled data labels unlabeled data, and a student trained on both becomes the next teacher. Expert Iteration (Anthony et al., 2017) and STaR (Zelikman et al., 2022) apply the same pattern to search and reasoning, and RoboCat (Bousmalis et al., 2024) applies it to robot data collection. Each round improves behavior and helps the next learner through the data it generates, while the recipe that generates, labels or filters, and trains on the data stays fixed. When the model also judges its own outputs, as in Self-Rewarding Language Models (Yuan et al., 2024), the evaluator changes with training, but the judging prompt and the training recipe do not.

Continual learning.

Continual, or lifelong, learning asks how a learner that meets a sequence of tasks can reuse what it learned on earlier ones (Thrun and Mitchell, 1995) without forgetting them (Parisi et al., 2019). Backward and forward transfer (Lopez-Paz and Ranzato, 2017) measure how learning one task changes performance on earlier and later ones. Backward transfer thus measures retention, whereas forward transfer as defined there is performance on a new task before training on it; LIBERO (Liu et al., 2023a), a benchmark of sequential robot manipulation tasks, instead scores forward transfer by success on each new task averaged over its training, which is closer to a later-learning benefit. Replay, regularization, and other protections against forgetting are usually chosen by designers, so continual learning defines a setting that an RSI system must also handle (Section 4.2) rather than a way of improving the improver.

Meta-learning.

Meta-learning trains over many tasks so that new tasks are learned faster (Thrun and Pratt, 1998), and what it learns decides which claim it can support. An initialization from which a few gradient steps solve a new task, as in MAML (Finn et al., 2017), is an acquired artifact and supports a later-learning benefit. Other methods learn part of the learning procedure: recurrent policies whose activations carry out adaptation (Duan et al., 2016; Wang et al., 2016), a dynamics prior that adapts online, as in GrBAL (Nagabandi et al., 2019), learned optimizers (Andrychowicz et al., 2016), and evolved or learned losses (Houthooft et al., 2018; Yu et al., 2018; Bechtle et al., 2021). When a learner guided by such a part learns better than a matched learner guided by the original, the comparison supports procedure improvement. LMPC (Liang et al., 2024) is a boundary case: it fine-tunes the model that writes reward code on past teaching sessions so that later teaching needs fewer turns, but that code drives a model-predictive controller directly rather than a training run, so the gain lies in how behavior is constructed. The outer meta-training procedure that produces the part (a gradient loop, an evolutionary search, or a fine-tuning recipe) is specified by its designers and is not changed by the revisions it produces, so learning reaches the procedure but not the process that produces it.

Automated machine learning.

AutoML searches over architectures, hyperparameters, and training pipelines (Hutter et al., 2019), and AutoML-Zero extends the search to learning algorithms (Real et al., 2020); a found configuration that teaches matched learners better supports procedure improvement. Some searchers learn from their own record: the controller of neural architecture search (Zoph and Le, 2017) is trained with reinforcement learning on the validation accuracy of the architectures it generates. Because it edits the learner rather than the rules for collecting, judging, or updating, it is a learned part of the procedure, like the skill editor of PRACTICE (Bai et al., 2026), and the rule that trains it stays fixed.

Where recursion adds a requirement.

RSI moves the process that produces revisions inside as well, as the two links of Section 2.2 require. The requirement is self-referential, as in the Gödel machine (Schmidhuber, 2003), STOP (Zelikman et al., 2024), the Darwin Gödel Machine (Zhang et al., 2026c), and Gödel Agent (Yin et al., 2025), which let the improving process act on its own code. Generalized Agent Iteration places iterative policy improvement and RSI in one formal framework, in which whether the improvement mechanism sits inside the agent separates the two, and whether the evaluation standard is grounded outside it decides whether goals can drift (Tang et al., 2026). The criterion is therefore which component changes with the revision history, not whether some outer recipe stays fixed. In the meta-learning and AutoML methods above, the component that produces revisions of the learning procedure does not change; an improver fine-tuned by a fixed recipe on its own repair histories does change and can satisfy R1, which the frozen-knowledge comparison of Section 4.4 tests. Conversely, an improver whose code changes in every round satisfies neither link if the changes come from a designer or from a search that learns nothing from earlier rounds.

## 2.4 Facing Physical Limits

Table 1 lists the terms we use to judge how closely a study is tied to physical experience. Hardware interaction connects learning directly to physical consequences, but a consequence still needs a diagnosis and a reliable judge. A binary failure label on a grasp may not distinguish pose error from slip, whereas force measurements or contact history may separate the two. Simulation, learned prediction, and formal checks represent selected consequences whose adequacy must be tested at the decision they support. Simulation is cheap and repeatable but omits some physical effects, whereas hardware captures real contact, sensing, and wear but allows few trials, so the two settings support different conclusions about cost, transfer, and the comparisons a study can afford.

Because practice changes its own conditions (Section 1), returning to a usable starting state may require a reset policy or human assistance; early work on autonomous real-world RL already named resets, onboard perception, and rewards without instrumentation as the ingredients that continued learning needs (Zhu et al., 2020). Moving knowledge to another robot adds a further gap, because reuse across bodies needs compatible coordinates, timing, sensing, and reachable effects. A study’s connection to physical experience is therefore a matter of degree. It depends on the success evaluation (who or what judges whether an attempt succeeded), on interaction coverage and recoverability, and on whether successive trials remain comparable. The success evaluation may rely on the same evaluator that supplied the learning signal or on one independent of it, a difference that Section 4.1 shows to matter. Table 1 contrasts these conditions with those of digital self-improvement, Section 4.3 develops their consequences for continued learning, and Table 18 in Appendix A shows what evidence each robot domain contributes.

These conditions weigh on each paradigm of Section 2.3 in a different place. Meta-learning needs a distribution of training tasks, and on hardware every sampled task costs trials, resets, and supervision; domain-adaptive meta-learning meta-trains on previously collected human and robot demonstrations, and LMPC collected its training data in simulation. Eureka and DrEureka run their reward search in simulation, where many candidates can be trained in parallel, and DrEureka then deploys the resulting policy on hardware (Ma et al., 2024b). For RSI, the bottleneck shifts to revision histories: an improver learns from them, and each entry needs at least one learning round judged by physical trials, so these histories accumulate more slowly than the task samples of meta-learning or the transitions of RL, and no executable test settles whether a revision helped. Simulation, stored traces, and learned models can screen candidate revisions before hardware trials, but their adequacy for that screening is itself a claim to test (Section 4.4).

## 2.5 Benchmarking Self-Improvement

Most comparisons in this review run in simulation, and the benchmarks differ in what they stress (Table 5). LIBERO (Liu et al., 2023a) supplies sequential manipulation suites, and LIBERO-PRO (Zhou et al., 2025b) perturbs manipulated objects, initial states, task instructions, and environments to test whether behavior depends on the original cues; LIBERO-Plus (Fei et al., 2025) varies seven factors, including camera viewpoint, initial robot state, and sensor noise. RoboCasa (Nasiriany et al., 2024) broadens household task coverage, and RoboTwin 2.0 (Chen et al., 2025c) and RoboDojo (Chen et al., 2026g) cover bimanual manipulation. VLABench (Zhang et al., 2025d) and Meta-World (Yu et al., 2019) supply further manipulation suites, and SIMPLER (Li et al., 2024) mirrors real robot setups for policy evaluation. Navigation studies use Habitat (Savva et al., 2019) and R2R-CE (Krantz et al., 2020), and household agents use EmbodiedBench (Yang et al., 2025). Except for LIBERO’s lifelong protocol, which scores transfer and forgetting along a task sequence, these suites test a system at one point in time, and a claim about improvement across rounds still needs a protocol on top that fixes what carries over between rounds and what stays held out (Section 4.2). A recent benchmark prototype moves in this direction: it provides no in-domain demonstrations, carries experience over between requests within a deployment, and names how quickly an agent becomes competent in a deployment as a quantity of interest (Cai et al., 2026). PhysRSI-eval (Li et al., 2026j) fixes what carries over between rounds, in simulation. Six model-and-configuration systems revise a persistent workspace of code, tools and notes with frozen weights, for up to 17 graded rounds on each of 18 RoboDojo tasks, and each system is scored on five axes: first-attempt score, how early gains appear, attained score, physical budget saved, and the share of first-round failures later solved. The axes need not agree: Claude Opus 5.5 attains a higher score than GPT-6.1 Sol but scores lower on gain timing and budget saved. In a six-task ablation with GPT-6 Astra, withholding all information from earlier rounds lowers success over rounds 4–6 from 15 of 36 attempts to 3 of 36.

A second group of benchmarks targets the components on which self-improvement relies. FailBench (Navasardyan et al., 2026) tests success detectors on 2,197 real and simulated manipulation attempts, where the best of 13 VLM detectors reaches 0.77 balanced accuracy. CaliBench (Sadeghi et al., 2026) tests whether the outcome frequencies of video world models are calibrated. MC-EvoBench (Song et al., 2026a) fixes the controls that a transfer claim needs, and RLE-Bench (Ma et al., 2026) holds out robustness perturbations that coding agents cannot query. RoboCoach-Bench (Lei et al., 2026) collects 32 simulated tasks for agents that write and revise training code. Digital benchmarks of recursive self-improvement isolate related questions. PAST-Bench (Xue et al., 2026a) turns retained experience on and off across ordered task sequences, and RSIBench-Data (Meng et al., 2026a) has agents revise training-data strategies for a fixed model. RSIGym (Meng et al., 2026b) measures the fraction of the remaining performance gap that agents close across benchmarks.

Hardware evaluations instead use task suites built by each study, and their budgets vary widely, from a handful of trials per task, as in PhysEvo’s 25 trials over five real tasks (Tian et al., 2026), to hundreds of evaluation episodes, as in the more than 600 pairwise episodes of RoboArena (Atreya et al., 2025). Most controlled downstream comparisons concern manipulation; navigation, locomotion, flight, and robotic laboratories support particular mechanisms without testing each condition across robot bodies (Table 18). Simulation results support mechanism comparisons at scale, whereas hardware results show what survives real contact, sensing, and resets (Section 2.4). Because tasks, trial counts, and metrics differ, results are compared within each study rather than across studies; Appendix B records the settings behind selected numbers.

Most studies report success rate, the fraction of trials that reach a task-defined goal as judged by a person, a scripted check, or a learned detector. Some give partial credit for progress through task stages, which needs a different statistical model than binary success. Others add efficiency during execution, such as cycle time in HIL-SERL (Luo et al., 2025) or throughput in RECAP (Physical Intelligence et al., 2026).

Learning efficiency is reported as the time, episodes, or supervision needed to reach a level of performance. ConRFT (Chen et al., 2025d) reaches 96.3% average success on eight real tasks after 45–90 minutes of online fine-tuning, and VLAC (Zhai et al., 2025) raises real success from about 30% to 90% within 200 episodes. ENPIRE (Xiao et al., 2026b) reports the time its robot fleet needs to reach a target, and LMPC (Liang et al., 2024) counts chat turns per successful teaching session.

Retention asks whether earlier competence survives later learning or deployment, and a later-learning benefit on a new task is forward transfer. ACCRUE (Kim et al., 2026) reports transfer as a cut in real-robot training time for later tasks, CIDER (Li et al., 2026d) reports forward transfer of an accumulated policy, and MC-EvoBench sets out the controls that such a claim needs.

Resource use is counted in robot hours, demonstrations, interventions, compute, or model tokens. CARD (Sun et al., 2024) needs about 14K tokens per task against about 663K for Eureka, and ENPIRE’s fleet scaling shortens the time to target at lower per-robot utilization and faster-than-linear token growth; because studies count different parts of this work, their costs are not directly comparable (Section 4.1).

\physicalaisectionaccentPAIBlue

## 3 Mechanisms toward Physical RSI

This section reviews physical self-improvement by what a system actually changes, following the ladder of Section 2.2. Section 3.1 covers changes to the learner $L$ , kept in policies, world models, or external knowledge; a policy trained with relabeled targets, a reward, or an optimization algorithm belongs there, because those rules stay fixed. Section 3.2 covers changes to the rules themselves, the learning procedure $P$ that collects, judges, and updates, and Section 3.3 covers changes to the improver $I^{K}$ that revises that procedure (notation of Section 2.2). Section 3.4 adds no fourth component: it asks whether these layers close into a recursive relation, in which the improver, once improved, helps produce its own next improvement. A system that serves several roles is discussed mainly where its experiment tests it, with cross-references elsewhere; Table 11 collects the systems that describe themselves as recursive self-improvement.

## 3.1 Improving Policies, World Models, and Knowledge

This section reviews the base loop of physical self-improvement: experience improves what the robot does, and the data, models, and skills it leaves behind can help later learners. These are the first two claims of Section 2.2, behavioral improvement and later-learning benefit. We follow the change to where it is kept, in a policy (Section 3.1.1), a world model (Section 3.1.2), or memories, skills, and code (Section 3.1.3). The learning procedure that produces these changes stays fixed here; where a study also changes how experience is collected, judged, or turned into updates, that part appears in Section 3.2.

## 3.1.1 Policy Updates

The most direct way for experience to help is to change the policy itself, and on the path to RSI the policy is also the most common target of a revised learning procedure. A deployed policy determines both what the robot does and which situations later appear in its training data, so the question to ask of new data is what learning signal they contain. Four kinds of signal recur. Relabeling reinterprets behavior that has already happened: an attempt that pushed the cup aside instead of grasping it is still a correct example of how to push the cup aside. A reward scores attempted outcomes so that optimization can prefer better ones. A correction supplies an action that the policy did not take, typically from a person who intervenes. Replay reuses earlier experience to preserve or recover previously learned behavior (Figure 5). Each signal fits a different failure.

1(hgRelabeling

Pushed aside, not graspedg

e.g., hindsight replay, SOAR
2(hgReward

e.g., SI-EFM, SARM2
3(hgCorrection

Supply a missing actiong

e.g., HIL-SERL, Max-Q
4(hgReplay

Earlier and new experienceg

Retain earlier behaviorg

e.g., Memory Anchors, CIDER

A signal helps only when an optimizer or selector can absorb it and turn it into an executable action.

Figure 5: Four teaching signals from physical experience. Each column follows the grasp example: what was experienced, what the signal changes in the training data, and representative systems from this section.

From practice to training targets.

A recorded trajectory becomes useful for learning only once a goal, reward, or action label is attached to it. Hindsight experience replay (Andrychowicz et al., 2017) relabels an attempt with the goal it actually achieved, and SOAR (Zhou et al., 2025c) carries this achieved-goal relabeling to goal-conditioned imitation. Earlier large-scale systems such as MT-Opt (Kalashnikov et al., 2022) shared physical outcomes across twelve tasks; pretrained generalists change the starting point, because practice can refine behavior that is already useful, and value-based fine-tuning must then keep offline estimates calibrated, as Cal-QL (Nakamoto et al., 2023) does in simulation. RoboCat (Bousmalis et al., 2024) closes the resulting cycle: a generalist adapted to a target task from demonstrations collects trajectories that, mixed with retained multi-task data, retrain the generalist offline. RoboCat also relabels unsuccessful episodes with the goals they reached, whereas SOAR retrains only on attempts that its fixed VLM judges successful (Appendix B).

Adding more of the robot’s own data does not necessarily improve the targets it learns from. In their study of mostly single-task policies on five simulated and two real manipulation tasks, Mirchandani et al. (2025) find that added autonomous data yield modest gains that saturate and, over two to four collection rounds, can regress. On two of the tasks, human demonstrations started from conditions where rollouts had failed help more than randomly chosen ones, whereas autonomous collection from the same conditions offers no comparable advantage. More state diversity in demonstration data does not always help, whereas inconsistent actions often hurt (Belkhale et al., 2023). These results separate two needs, finding the difficult conditions and supplying useful actions there; repeating the policy’s familiar successes addresses neither.

From feedback to better actions.

Relabeling cannot supply an action the policy never produced, but an incomplete attempt can still reveal partial progress, and a person can replace part of it with a corrective motion. SI-EFM (Ghasemipour et al., 2025) takes the first route: a frozen model of the steps remaining to a goal rewards each step that brings the robot closer, and policy gradients then learn from incomplete real-robot attempts without new demonstrations, though only among variations that the policy actually tries. FAR (Hao et al., 2026) uses failed attempts in a different way: it turns them into preference data that steers retries away from the failed behavior and adds successful recoveries to later training, with gains in simulation and on real manipulation tasks.

Corrections take the second route. HIL-SERL (Luo et al., 2025) builds on SERL (Luo et al., 2024), which combines off-policy learning, learned reward classifiers, and reset policies, and adds online human interventions so that corrective transitions inform the critic that guides the actor. RECAP (Physical Intelligence et al., 2026) conditions the policy on the advantage of each action, fitted from deployment trajectories and expert corrections. Max-Q selective imitation (Wang and Wang, 2026) regresses the actor toward whichever action a Monte Carlo chunk critic scores higher: its own action or a replay-buffer action, which is often an intervention. The target therefore shifts from corrections to the policy’s own behavior as it improves. On real USB insertion, it reaches 99% success after half an hour, whereas HIL-SERL needs about five hours to reach its 100% ceiling (each at its best checkpoint). SiLRI (Zhao et al., 2025) treats human interventions as possibly suboptimal: it poses real-world RL as a constrained problem whose bound at each state is set by the uncertainty of the interventions, solved with a state-wise Lagrange multiplier, and reaches 90% success on real manipulation tasks in at most half the time HIL-SERL needs. The same recorded transition can become a value target, a weighted action target, or a conditioning variable, as RAPolicy (Yang et al., 2026a), SI-EFM, and RECAP illustrate, and which of these the learner receives changes what it learns.

Two studies keep a pretrained VLA frozen during online learning and train an added module alongside it. BEE (Zhao et al., 2026b) uses the consistency of human corrections to constrain RL updates to a residual policy around a frozen VLA. On three real tasks and one simulated task, it reaches 91.2% average success against 57.5% for an RL baseline at a matched online-data budget. RL Token (Xu et al., 2026b) instead adapts a VLA to expose a compact readout, keeps it frozen, and trains a small actor–critic on that readout online. On four real tasks, minutes to a few hours of practice speed up the hardest phase by up to 3 $\times$ and raise success as judged by a human operator, for example from 20% to 65% on screw installation. Outside manipulation, SELFI (Hirose et al., 2024) fine-tunes a pretrained navigation policy with online model-free RL whose Q-values keep the model-based objective of its offline pretraining, improving collision avoidance and, in a user study, social compliance in real environments.

Reward-guided post-training of VLAs has scaled mainly in simulation, while its most reliable results come from smaller hardware studies. VLA-RL (Lu et al., 2025) on LIBERO (Liu et al., 2023a) and $\pi_{\texttt{RL}}$ (Chen et al., 2025b) run in simulation, and FLaRe (Hu et al., 2025a) and SimpleVLA-RL (Li et al., 2026c) add real-robot results. In eight-task hardware studies, ConRFT (Chen et al., 2025d) reaches 96.3% average success after 45–90 minutes of online fine-tuning and RL-100 (Lei et al., 2025a) succeeds in 1,000 of 1,000 evaluation episodes. Task-specific hardware studies such as GR-RL (Li et al., 2025b) for shoe lacing and VLA-Precision (Su et al., 2026) for chemistry tasks push precision further (Appendix B). Autonomous real-world RL also reaches beyond tabletop arms: on Spot mobile manipulators, task-relevant autonomy, behavior priors, and generic rewards reach 80% average success on four tasks (Mendonca et al., 2024).

Without per-task success checks, such training needs rewards that generalize, and the stricter test of a learned reward is the behavior it teaches rather than its prediction accuracy. Pretrained vision and language models supply them as zero-shot similarities in VLM-RM (Rocamonde et al., 2024) and RoboCLIP (Sontakke et al., 2023), both evaluated in simulation, or as embedding distances learned from human video in VIP (Ma et al., 2023b) and LIV (Ma et al., 2023a), both with real-robot evaluations. ReWiND (Zhang et al., 2025c), RoboReward (Lee et al., 2026b), and VLAC (Zhai et al., 2025) judge learned rewards by how they transfer and by the behavior they teach; VLAC raises real success on four tasks from about 30% to 90% within 200 episodes. Finer progress distinctions preserve more of what incomplete attempts teach: SARM2 (Chen et al., 2026e) conditions its reward model on the estimated task stage, adapts it once, and holds it fixed while the SPIRAL loop learns residual corrections to a frozen VLA. On crumpled garments, three SPIRAL rounds raise success from 4/12 to 8/12, while the matched loop with only a sparse success reward falls from 4/12 to 2/12.

From proposals to policies.

Feedback that can rank behavior leaves a second choice: where the improvement should live. Choosing among proposals can improve execution without retraining, as V-GPS (Nakamoto et al., 2024) does by reranking a frozen policy’s proposals with an offline value function; PA-RL (Mark et al., 2024) also improves sampled actions under a critic and trains the policy on them with a supervised loss. Learned judges can steer a frozen policy in the same way. UF-OPS (Attarian et al., 2026) trains success and time-to-success verifiers once on the policy’s labeled rollouts and selects among sampled action chunks at run time, with reported gains of 25 to 80 percentage points on five real Aloha tasks. World-Coherent Decoding (Zhang et al., 2026b) learns during deployment to rank a frozen world action model’s sampled futures by the mismatch between predicted and observed frames. In RoboTwin 2.0 simulation, under the authors’ limited-randomization protocol, it raises Hard-split success from 55.80% to 60.90%. Keeping scoring, improvement, and policy change separate avoids the need to adapt policy gradients to each action representation, whether iteratively denoised chunks in Diffusion Policy (Chi et al., 2023), discretized tokens in OpenVLA (Kim et al., 2024), or flow matching in $\pi_{0}$ (Black et al., 2025). Selection gains can stay in the selector or be absorbed into the generator. VERITAS (Zhang and Shah, 2026) behavior-clones trajectories steered toward agreement with a visual path proposed by a VLM. Q-Planning (Giridhar et al., 2026) instead leaves the cloned policy unchanged and updates a Q-function from successful and failed rollouts. On its two real-robot tasks, terminal rewards come from human episode labels and success is measured on the collection episodes themselves.

Both routes stay bounded by their proposals: if the generator never produces a grasp that avoids the slip, no selector can recover one. A residual policy modifies the proposed motion while keeping the base policy as a prior. ResiP (Ankile et al., 2025) trains one in simulation and then distills it into a vision policy co-trained with real demonstrations. DSRL (Wagenmaker et al., 2025) instead steers the input noise of a frozen diffusion policy, searching only within its learned distribution. DPPO (Ren et al., 2025) fine-tunes the diffusion policy itself and transfers a simulation-trained assembly policy to hardware. Expanding proposals helps only if the resulting experience teaches the policy where it needs help, which PLD (Xiao et al., 2026a) tests directly. A residual-RL specialist takes over after the generalist has run for a sampled number of steps, and the hybrid trajectories are distilled back. In real cube pickup, a PLD specialist and an RLPD (Ball et al., 2023) specialist both reach full success within two hours from the same initialization, and each supplies 200 successful trajectories to the same distillation. The resulting generalists nevertheless differ, succeeding in 30 and 16 of 30 trials, respectively, because only the PLD data include recoveries from the corner states that the learner itself visits; collector success alone therefore does not reveal training value. Delay creates a further mismatch: Real-Time EXPO-FT (Dong et al., 2026) edits the action chunks of a slower VLA with the latest observation and selects among candidates with a critic, because an action can become wrong between prediction and execution. Selection suffices when useful behavior already lies in the proposal set; otherwise the generator must be optimized, corrected, steered, or fine-tuned, and distillation retains corrections that should become habitual (Table 6).

From single runs to lifelong practice.

At scale, RoboNet (Dasari et al., 2020) pools trajectories across laboratories and embodiments, and Learning While Deploying (Wang et al., 2026j) repeatedly updates one generalist from rollouts and interventions across sixteen robots. SOP (Pan et al., 2026) streams on-policy experience and human interventions from a fleet to a cloud learner and reports real-world performance that scales near-linearly with the number of robots. OpenBot-Fleet (Müller et al., 2024) sends learned navigation policies back to user-operated robots. More sources widen coverage only where observations, actions, and task definitions align. Practice also needs a way back to a useful starting state: Leave No Trace (Eysenbach et al., 2018) learns forward and reset policies in simulation, ReLMM (Sun et al., 2022) learns reset-free on a physical mobile manipulator, and MEDAL++ (Sharma et al., 2023) and RoboFuME (Yang et al., 2024a) need only occasional manual resets on real robots.

Against forgetting, capacity can be allocated or routed, as AtomicVLA (Zhang et al., 2026g) grows expert modules for atomic skills and CLARE (Römer et al., 2026b) expands and selects adapters without task labels, or adaptation can be combined with rehearsal, as in LifelongVLA (He et al., 2026b) and iManip (Zheng et al., 2025), or with targeted replay, as in Memory Anchors (Du et al., 2026). Separation costs capacity and routing; retaining experience, as in gradient episodic memory (Lopez-Paz and Ranzato, 2017), costs storage but can also speed later tasks (Xie and Finn, 2022). CIDER (Li et al., 2026d) freezes the accumulated policy as a teacher before each new task and alternates current-task RL with distillation on archived states; over six real tasks and three runs each, the retained policy needs no more training steps to reach the success criterion than the same RL agent from the common initialization, and fewer on some tasks. Replay and distillation assume a fixed action interface: FAN (Hong et al., 2026a) shows that updating action-normalization statistics between tasks shifts the coordinates shared by training and execution; statistics frozen after a small task-independent calibration give the highest average score across four real task streams.

Synthesis

Different failures call for different teaching signals

• 

Match the signal to the failure. More successes supply neither a missing action nor a finer distinction.

• 

The signal must reach an executable action. An optimizer, selector, or action interface must absorb it, and changed behavior calls for rechecking coverage, retention, and the interface.

• 

Behavioral gains are well established; later-learning benefits are rarer. Hardware comparisons establish behavioral gains; RoboCat, PLD, and CIDER also report later-learning benefits.

• 

Choose the signal from recorded failures. Compare a procedure that picks relabeling, a better reward, a correction, or replay from recorded failures with a fixed choice under the same budget (Sections 3.2.1 and 4).

## 3.1.2 World Models for Prediction and Practice

Physical trials are slow and costly, so a natural way to stretch them is to learn a model of the world and practice inside it. A world model predicts how a physical state may change after an action; for a grasp, it might predict whether the object will stay between the fingers when the gripper closes. The prediction can serve as a plan that must be translated into motion, as imagined experience from which a policy is trained, or as predicted features supplied to a policy. Each role tests a different form of usability: success in one role says little about the others. Generative video models offer all three interfaces: a generated video serves as a plan in UniPi (Du et al., 2023), as a controllable simulator in UniSim (Yang et al., 2024b) and, learned without action labels, in Genie (Bruce et al., 2024), and as policy-training data in DreamGen (Jang et al., 2025). Cosmos (NVIDIA et al., 2025) develops world foundation models for physical applications, WISA (Wang et al., 2025a) and PISA (Li et al., 2025a) target physical consistency, and SPIRAL (Yang et al., 2026d) (unrelated to the SPIRAL policy loop of Section 3.1.1) updates its generator with a critic that checks action adherence and physical realism. A world model can also enter the learning procedure by judging candidate policies or changes before they reach hardware (Sections 3.2.2 and 4.4).

Using models to plan and train.

Classical model-based methods supplied the main ingredients. Dyna (Sutton, 1990) interleaves real and modeled experience, PILCO (Deisenroth and Rasmussen, 2011) and PETS (Chua et al., 2018) account for model uncertainty, MBPO (Janner et al., 2019) keeps imagined rollouts short to limit compounding error, self-supervised visual planning (Ebert et al., 2017) plans through learned video prediction, and latent world models (Ha and Schmidhuber, 2018; Hafner et al., 2020) train policies in compact dynamics, as DayDreamer (Wu et al., 2023) did on physical robots. A model’s value for control need not track its prediction accuracy (Lambert et al., 2020; Farahmand et al., 2017; Grimm et al., 2020).

Planning first exposes an interface problem: a predicted video of the gripper closing is not yet a robot command. SILVR (Luo et al., 2026a) translates video plans through a separately trained inverse-dynamics model, and on hardware its successful executions update only the task video planner. In its simulated Meta-World (Yu et al., 2019) ablation over twelve unseen tasks, updating both planner and translator reaches 44.2% success, against 26.8% with the translator frozen: an improving planner can be held back by a translator that cannot express its new motions. The hardware counterpart of this comparison remains to be run.

A model used for training shifts the burden to policy optimization, and its gain depends on how far the policy may optimize against the model. RISE (Yang et al., 2026b) freezes its learned dynamics and value models, limits imagined interactions to two steps, and updates an advantage-conditioned VLA; its hardware comparison reimplements RECAP (Physical Intelligence et al., 2026) with the same $\pi_{0.5}$ (Physical Intelligence et al., 2025) initialization, because the $\pi_{0.6}$ model used by RECAP is not available. PlayWorld (Yin et al., 2026b) trains only the small DSRL (Wagenmaker et al., 2025) policy that chooses its generator’s input noise against the fitted world model, rewarded by a progress estimator trained on demonstrations; with twenty hardware trials per checkpoint, its best reported task improves by 65 percentage points over the base policy, yet later optimization inside the model can reduce real success. WMPO (Zhu et al., 2025b), Ctrl-World (Guo et al., 2025), ProphRL (Zhang et al., 2025b), and World-Gymnast (Sharma et al., 2026) likewise improve VLAs with imagined rollouts. Fine-tuning on imagined successes raises Ctrl-World’s policy success by 44.7 percentage points, and ProphRL, which runs flow-policy GRPO inside an action-conditioned video model pretrained on more than 31M trajectories, raises overall success on four real UR30e tasks by 24.6 to 30.0 percentage points over the starting policies of three VLA backbones (Appendix B). Whether new physical experience should also repair the practice model is a further choice, one that changes what later updates learn from; Section 3.2.3 follows VLAW’s loop of this kind. Figure 6 separates these decisions, and Table 7 records what the main model-assisted studies update, hold fixed, and compare.

Figure 6: Where models, policies, and physical data change. Learning loops of SILVR, RISE, PlayWorld, and VLAW (Luo et al., 2026a; Yang et al., 2026b; Yin et al., 2026b; Guo et al., 2026).

Feeding models the right data.

Whether new rollouts can repair a model depends on what they cover for the component that consumes them. A contact model needs partial contacts, slips, and recoveries, not only successes: PlayWorld collects such evidence through autonomous play, SILVR through task-directed rollouts from its current planner, and Motus2 (Bi et al., 2026) routes curated successes to action generation and failed or suboptimal interactions to dynamics or value prediction, freezing its video backbone and evaluator during later policy optimization. When natural failures miss the needed corrections, XPACE (Wei et al., 2026a) constructs them: its world model renders deviations from expert demonstrations followed by recoveries, and fine-tuning on a mixture containing 8% of these examples yields hardware gains across three tasks evaluated with twenty trials per method. A failed contact may be valuable for predicting consequences, while only a validated recovery should supervise the action policy.

Dynamics can also change. Roughly 100 seconds of real quadrotor flight support online identification of a residual dynamics model and in-flight policy updates (Ren et al., 2026); Variational Neural Dynamics (Xing et al., 2026) instead infers which recurring operating condition applies from recent interaction; and Self-adapting Robotic Agents (Domberg and Schildbach, 2026) detect out-of-distribution events from prediction residuals and trigger fine-tuning. D-World (Wen and Wang, 2026), which its authors describe as recursive self-improvement, keeps an explicit digital twin that a frozen language model revises after each executed action chunk and accepts a revision only when replayed residuals do not grow; on a real dual-arm robot, one revision reduced a rendered-depth residual from 22.4 to 1.5 mm. After damage, Bongard et al. (2006) revise a model of the robot’s body and search it for a new gait, Intelligent Trial and Error (Cully et al., 2015) searches a precomputed repertoire of behaviors, and GrBAL (Nagabandi et al., 2019) adapts a meta-learned dynamics prior (Section 2.3). Which route saves physical trials depends on what useful structure survives the change.

Shaping what models predict.

For control, a representation must distinguish futures that imply different actions, which pixel-accurate prediction neither guarantees nor requires. Joint-embedding predictive architectures predict in learned feature spaces and plan toward goals there, as V-JEPA 2-AC (Assran et al., 2025) and DINO-WM (Zhou et al., 2025a) do, and D-JEPA (Liu et al., 2026e) aligns competing predicted futures with the candidate that executes best. Physical properties must remain readable: in PokeWorld (Tan et al., 2026a), predicting touch makes stiffness decodable whereas merely supplying touch as an input does not, and PSG-JEPA (Yan et al., 2026) adds proprioceptive objectives where forward prediction alone omits such structure. DreamerV3 (Hafner et al., 2025), which reconstructs observations, and TD-MPC2 (Hansen et al., 2024), which does not, bracket the design space. Retrieved history in Mem-World (Zheng et al., 2026b), force–torque prediction in MuSe (Clark et al., 2026), and predicted fingertip wrenches in TORL-VLA (Zheng et al., 2026a) supply distinctions that camera data alone can miss.

The information must also reach the policy through a usable interface, and the model must respond correctly to commands. GigaBrain-0.5M* (GigaBrain Team et al., 2026) conditions action learning on predicted future-state tokens, whereas VLAW turns predicted futures into reward-filtered training data; a conditioned policy may ignore or over-rely on auxiliary tokens, whereas synthetic data may amplify prediction errors. WorldEcho (Chen et al., 2026f) shows that models trained only on expert trajectories, given commands outside that distribution, either ignore the commanded motion or produce visually invalid rollouts, and CausalWorld (Ahmed et al., 2021) lets physical and visual variables be changed independently. Across embodiments, particle-based action representations (He et al., 2026c) offer a shared geometric interface, while XEWorld (Chen et al., 2026i) finds that current models bind joint commands more readily to familiar appearance than to transferable kinematics. A visually plausible model may still lack decision-relevant state, a usable interface, or correct responses to intervention.

Judging models by their use.

Failure can enter at four links between a model and its policy: transition prediction, outcome scoring, policy optimization, or translation into executable motion. End-to-end hardware results, as in RISE and PlayWorld, measure joint usefulness but do not say which link broke, so evaluation needs both final performance and comparisons that change one link at a time. DexTouch-WM (Qin et al., 2026) shows why. Its world model trained on mixed human and robot data improves held-out prediction and, on average, agreement between policy rankings in imagination and on hardware, yet replacing 100 of 200 real training trajectories with synthetic counterparts generated by this model lowers the mean physical partial-credit score for each of three policy families (FTP-1, $\pi_{0.5}$ , and X-VLA) across four tasks. A model can become a better surrogate evaluator and still produce a worse training mixture.

Other methods change how predictions are consumed. WISE (Zhang et al., 2026a) calls a frozen world model only at states where interaction matters and bounds the imagined horizon, and HaWMPO (Chen et al., 2026j) trains a hallucination estimator on discrepancies between generated and observed outcomes and uses it to soften rewards from unreliable action chunks. How prediction is queried also matters: in CaliBench (Sadeghi et al., 2026), individually plausible videos can have poorly calibrated outcome frequencies, and in a simulated differential-drive study (Raghavan and Singh, 2026), a 20-step rollout without intermediate measurements picks a different state estimator than closed-loop tracking in 18 of 24 sensing conditions, whereas replay error under the recorded schedule disagrees in five. Evaluation should reproduce the actions, horizon, and observation schedule of the policy’s use.

Synthesis

A useful model preserves distinctions the controller can exploit

• 

Cover and preserve what the decision requires. Physical data must reach the transitions, contacts, and recoveries the consumer lacks, and prediction must preserve distinctions that change outcomes.

• 

Prediction helps only through its consumer. Predictive gains help control only through a suitable evaluator, learner, and action interface.

• 

Value belongs to the full chain. Hardware gains such as VLAW’s belong to combined pipelines, and DexTouch-WM’s better surrogate evaluator yields worse training data.

• 

Let outcomes select the repair. Refitting the model, restricting imagination, and downweighting unreliable predictions each sit in a different system; Section 4.4 asks how physical outcomes could choose among them.

## 3.1.3 Memories, Skills, Code, and Runtimes

Not every useful change needs to enter a policy’s weights or a world model. A robot that misses grasps might remember that a particular object tends to slip, save a regrasp routine that worked, or add a monitor that interrupts the policy when slip is detected. We use memory for stored information that must be interpreted when it is used, skill for a callable behavior with an execution interface, and runtime or software for the logic that retrieves, composes, monitors, or repairs those behaviors (Figure 7). The same artifact can cross these boundaries (a recovery written down as text becomes a skill once a controller can execute it), but the distinction identifies who uses the change next; for all three, the question is whether a change kept outside model weights is usable. On the path to RSI, external knowledge is also where a system can keep the repair knowledge that recursion requires (Section 2.2).
