Figure 7: Three forms of external knowledge in the grasp example. Each panel shows one saved form of the slip repair and representative systems from this section; reuse on another robot first checks the interface.

Earlier systems kept different kinds of artifacts. Options (Sutton et al., 1999) encode temporally extended behaviors that a higher-level controller can call, and Horde (Sutton et al., 2011) learns many sensorimotor predictions in parallel. RoboEarth (Waibel et al., 2011) shares models and action recipes among robots. Code as Policies (Liang et al., 2023) writes programs against perception and control APIs, and Inner Monologue (Huang et al., 2023) feeds execution feedback back into planning. In digital environments, including games (Luo et al., 2026b), Voyager (Wang et al., 2024a) accumulates executable skills and Reflexion (Shinn et al., 2023) stores written reflections on earlier failures, showing how a frozen model can still take part in an agent that improves; the Weights or Skills? survey (Jena et al., 2026) reviews this broader choice. Physical reuse additionally needs valid sensing, control, and conditions of applicability.

Remembering experience.

Memory systems differ in what they store, how strongly they abstract it, through which interface it is read, and how long it persists, and a memory helps only if these choices preserve a relation that a later decision can exploit. Some systems keep the scene and past interaction themselves: RoboMemory (Lei et al., 2025b) and AllDayNav (Yin et al., 2026a) preserve continuity of the environment and of past interaction, and PhyAgentOS (Liu et al., 2026h) keeps sessions, evidence, recovered lessons, and capability descriptions as versioned records inside a runtime that also checks compatibility, supervises execution, and invokes recovery, so its gains belong to the integrated package.

Others distill experience into reusable guidance, which must then be checked against later conditions. Robo-Cortex (Chan et al., 2026) distills progress and failure histories into principles and induced navigation heuristics, evaluated mainly in unseen settings of the Habitat simulator (Savva et al., 2019) with a preliminary physical study of ten navigation goals. RoboHarn-Evo (Bao et al., 2026) also revises what it stores: physical feedback updates task- and action-level entries together with their stated applicability, and knowledge acquired in simulation reaches 41.7% mean zero-shot success on real tasks, rising to 62.5% after updates from real interaction. PhysMem (Li et al., 2026b) makes the check explicit: a frozen VLM planner commits a hypothesis about physical properties to memory only after targeted interaction verifies it. On simulated medium-difficulty brick insertion this reaches 76% success against 53% without memory and 23% when raw experience is retrieved directly (100 episodes per condition, three seeds), so retrieving raw experience can do worse than having no memory; its real xArm6 experiments report plan quality over 30-minute deployment sessions. PragmaBot (Qu et al., 2025) summarizes the successful episodes of a real legged manipulator into a retrievable memory, raising single-trial success over twelve scenarios, eight of them new but structurally similar, from 22% for a planner without memory to 80%. Storage is only half of reuse: a retriever must select the record, and a recipient must assign it the right role.

How long a memory lives also limits what it can show. With the foundation policy of Zeva (Chen et al., 2026a) fixed, removing its interaction memory lowers success by 10–20 percentage points on five real ChemLab-Evo tasks, but the store is cleared before each episode, so the benefit is adaptation across retries within an episode.

Reusing skills and code.

A one-off recovery becomes a reusable skill only when it is detached from the incident that produced it, with an implementation, a callable interface, and stated conditions and effects that let a planner place it in a new sequence. Implementations come from demonstrations in LOTUS (Wan et al., 2024) and Uni-Skill (Xie et al., 2026b), and from a person when the library cannot carry out the plan in HaLP (Parakh et al., 2024). The robot’s own execution supplies them in MobiAgent (Liu et al., 2026a) and ReSYNC (Li et al., 2026a). MobiAgent clusters verified deployment rollouts into atomic skills and raises real success on an Astribot S1 from 32.5% to 57.5%, and ReSYNC learns recovery policies together with the symbolic predicates and operators that let a planner compose them.

Code-writing agents often call two artifacts “skills”: a callable routine and guidance for constructing one. LRLL (Tziafas and Kasaei, 2024) distills simulated instruction–program pairs into callable Python APIs under a curriculum. ASPIRE (Lu et al., 2026a) retrieves guidance distilled from earlier program repairs when debugging a new controller; on three physical tasks whose embodiment and API differ from the simulation source, retrieval reduces the reasoning tokens spent on the real robot, and held-out success is unchanged on one task and improves on two. Reconstruct, Practice, Go Real (Wang et al., 2026i) revises symbolic skills and its system prompt during simulated practice, keeps a change only after a cross-task check, and reaches 95.0% against 75.5% for ASPIRE on held-out initializations of 22 simulated tasks; the frozen system succeeds in all 30 physical trials.

Libraries can also be built in advance and frozen, and the clearest results come from passing a frozen library to fresh or weaker agents. RATs (Zhang et al., 2026f) distills successful play into a code-skill library for simulated LIBERO-PRO tasks. EmbodiedEvo (Li et al., 2026g) keeps code skills and written lessons that raise fresh agents’ success on unseen layouts of the RoboDojo simulation benchmark (Chen et al., 2026g) from 8.50% to 30.17%, with similar success at 53.0% lower execution cost than the raw interaction history. Its frozen library also raises a weaker executor that failed to acquire it by 8.86 percentage points over no experience. PhysRSI-eval (Li et al., 2026j) freezes GPT-6 Astra workspaces after six rounds of revision on six RoboDojo tasks and tests each on five unseen layouts with no further revision; across 12 workspaces, the stored files alone succeed in 25 of 60 tests. Workspaces pooled from the other tasks also bring the first success earlier in three cases, for example from round 14 to round 7 for Claude Opus 5.5 on the Organize Table task. RoboSkill (Xie et al., 2026c) reports real-robot gains on its hard tasks, and Agent as Policy (Jia et al., 2026) shortens execution across repeated real trials. Embodied Turing Machines (Hu et al., 2026b) make every decision in code on simulated bimanual tasks (Table 11). A frozen library can also be written from demonstrations rather than practice. In an evaluation of GPT-6-Astra (Cai et al., 2026), the model studies a task’s training demonstrations and writes task guidance, motion references, executable helpers, and recovery procedures into a package that is frozen before testing and given to every fresh test session. On four simulated humanoid tasks of the SIMPLE benchmark, the package raises success from 70.8% to 90.8% (120 episodes per condition), with gains that depend on how closely the demonstrations cover the test tasks.

Studies that vary the retained artifact while holding its user fixed show most clearly what the artifact must encode. ReTrace (Yu et al., 2026a) lets a model controlling a real Franka arm trace a late failure to an earlier decision, test one correction per attempt, and keep supported corrections as a text skill. With skills that one model learned in a few attempts, a second executing model raises plug insertion and ball pouring from 0% to 80% and 100%, respectively, over ten real trials each. In a code-transfer study (Kuroki and Tang, 2026), with the coding model, cases, interfaces, and per-session limits held fixed, mean success on nine new tasks in the simulated RoboCasa environment (Nasiriany et al., 2024) is 45.2% without references, 41.5% with initial source programs, and 57.0% with execution-optimized ones: a retained artifact helps only if it encodes distinctions that the next synthesizer can exploit. Table 8 summarizes selected external-artifact updates of this section and their outcome checks.

One library across tasks; no test-time model

Code transferKuroki and Tang, 2026

 Program archive 

 synthesis rules

Simulated task execution

 Common benchmark checks

Nine new tasks; three synthesis runs

Even a correct skill fails if it is called under the wrong conditions, and some systems revise those conditions directly. VASO (Yang et al., 2026e) model-checks skill-based plans against temporal-logic specifications and turns counterexample traces into updates of each skill’s semantic contract. Across tasks for a Jackal ground robot and the PX4 autopilot, eleven specifications, and 400 generated plans, the optimized skills reach 97.2% (handcrafted alignment) or 96.8% (automatic) specification compliance; compliance holds relative to the encoded propositions, and unmodeled sensing or contact errors lie outside it. The conditions can also concern the robot itself. KnowBody (Lou et al., 2026) keeps an explicit, revisable model of how the robot’s body produces effects, with the VLM frozen, and rechecks knowledge that depends on a revised body estimate before reusing it. Over 32 fixed-budget trials on four real-robot tasks, it completes 75% against 25% for the native harness. With persistent updates, its planner rounds fall by 29–53% from the first to the fifth recorded success. Composition also creates handoffs, as when a placed mug hides the next object from the camera, so a skill’s stated effects should include the state it hands to its successor.

Revising the runtime.

The runtime selects context, monitors progress, interrupts, routes control to recoveries, and decides when the nominal policy may resume; revising it can change the effective agent while the weights of planner and controller stay fixed. The reported comparisons show that runtime components interact, and most of them are measured in simulation. SHAPER (Wang et al., 2026e) edits textual procedural guidance and a Python harness around a frozen planner and executor, and on VLABench (Zhang et al., 2025d) the two edits are not additive: the full configuration scores 34.50% against 28.25% for the seed, less than the skill-only and harness-only gains combined. Zetta (Ding et al., 2026) grows code-based critics that detect local failure signatures such as a slipping grasp and promotes a critic–recovery patch only after diagnostic replay and a fresh closed-loop rollout. Harness VLA (Zhang et al., 2026j) keeps a VLA frozen as a retryable contact primitive and learns from execution traces when it and a few analytic primitives apply. SHAPER and Zetta are evaluated in simulation; Harness VLA measures its gains in simulation, with demonstrations on two dual-arm Franka setups. Baton (Xu et al., 2026a) explores each subtask separately around a frozen VLA and stores memories of the transitions between subtasks; with exploration on one scene seed and evaluation on ten held-out seeds of 26 simulated RoboMemArena tasks, it reaches 63.5% task success against 46.1% for the strongest prior method, and the system is also run on a real PiPER-X arm.

Several systems revise programs and tools around a frozen model and gate what they admit. DynaHarness (Deng et al., 2026) admits a revision of the capabilities around a frozen $\pi_{0.5}$ only after paired regression checks, and its frozen final system reaches 75.2% on 800 newly sampled simulated LIBERO-PRO initial states against 17.5% for the bare policy. HarnessPAI (Wang et al., 2026g), evaluated only in simulation, has a coding agent revise a task program around a frozen action model and reaches 96.5% on simulated LIBERO-PRO tasks, on evaluation seeds that include its development seeds; fine-tuning $\pi_{0.5}$ on the harness’s successful rollouts raises held-out-seed success from 34.9% to 73.7%. URAI (Ge et al., 2026) has a frozen agent call validated tools one complete motion at a time, raising pooled success on simulated RoboDojo tasks from 18.0% under direct fingertip control to 53.0%; tool revision by its programming agent appears in an author-steered case study. RoboRSI (Wen et al., 2026), named for recursive self-improvement, confines each patch to the skill branch where a failure began and releases it only after review, tests, and replay; on three of its four simulation benchmarks, its gains are measured while it improves online on the evaluation tasks (Table 11). RACaP (Li et al., 2026k) replaces its parent system only when a candidate achieves strictly higher success under identical tasks, seeds, and budgets: over 596 simulated development episodes, nine of the 23 tested candidates were promoted, and the frozen result reaches 54.4% on the 90 LIBERO tasks it evolved on and 45.0% zero-shot on LIBERO-PRO. In DynaHarness, HarnessPAI, URAI, and RoboRSI, any agent that writes revisions is a fixed model, although HarnessPAI’s growing library of failure-to-repair notes changes what its program writer knows.

A different route trains the program writer itself. PyRUA-Lean (Si et al., 2026) fine-tunes a 4B model that composes robot primitives, including VLA policies, into Python cells on 45 simulated LIBERO-PRO task instances, raising its success on unseen instances from 18.8% to 44.1%.

Transferring across robots.

Moving knowledge to another robot can fail at each level of description. Saved as a task, the regrasp transfers only if the receiving robot can ground it in skills of its own, and saved as a named skill, it transfers only if that robot implements the same effect. Saved as an end-effector motion, it transfers only if both robots interpret the motion alike; saved as joint commands, it transfers only to an arm of the same kind. SayCan (Ichter et al., 2023) grounds skills by combining their usefulness for the task with their estimated chance of success, while Open X-Embodiment (Open X-Embodiment Collaboration et al., 2024) standardizes data from twenty-two embodiments but maps actions to a coarse common end-effector format that leaves coordinate frames unaligned, so the same numerical action can induce different motions on different robots. RoboFoundry (Liang et al., 2026) separates embodiment-invariant decisions from embodiment-specific execution so that evolved memories and recovery skills can transfer, and PhyAgentOS checks observations, action semantics, timing, capabilities, and safety configuration before building adapters; its validation on Franka, PIPER, and Dobot arms tests whether the interface carries over, not whether a saved lesson does. ASPIRE tests a lesson on a single receiving robot: guidance from simulated Franka repairs helps a coding agent debug programs for a bimanual robot with a different API, on which no simulated program runs unchanged. SHAPER, Zetta, and the code-transfer study move artifacts across simulated tasks but not across robots. Evaluation should hold the artifact or the adapter fixed while varying the other.

Synthesis

Reuse depends on the conditions saved with a repair

• 

Avoid rediscovering execution knowledge. Saved experience helps when the limitation is a recurring failure, a missing recovery or applicability condition, or runtime logic that defeats an adequate skill.

• 

Reuse requires retrieval and realization. A reusable artifact should therefore state its required observations, action semantics, preconditions, effects, and validation evidence, which later changes can make stale.

• 

Reuse by later learners is tested only in part. Most reported gains are behavioral improvements, often of integrated packages or in simulation, and Zeva’s memory benefit is episode-local. ASPIRE and the code-transfer study report benefits to later learning: a retained artifact helps a coding agent construct controllers for new tasks, on a second robot in ASPIRE’s case; RATs and EmbodiedEvo pass frozen libraries to other agents.

• 

Aging and revalidation. The open comparisons concern when a stored repair becomes stale, how to revalidate it after a change in object, tool, or robot, and whether accumulated repair knowledge improves the rule used on a later failure (Sections 3.3 and 4.4).

## 3.2 Improving Learning Procedures

The changes reviewed so far also alter what the next round of learning starts from. An improved policy reaches states that were previously inaccessible, so the data available for the next update change too. The resulting experience can expose distinctions missing from supervision; revised supervision changes what an update teaches; and the repair that follows can become evidence for constructing later updates. Along this chain, a change of any form can take on a new role in the learning procedure (Figure 4). Improving that procedure is where RSI would begin, because it changes how the robot learns rather than only what it does.

Reuse and procedure change can overlap, but they answer different questions: reuse asks whether a change is carried forward, whereas procedure change asks whether it alters collection, judgment, or updating for a later learner. We take the procedure’s decisions in turn: where the next experience comes from ( $c$ , Section 3.2.1), how outcomes are judged ( $e$ , Section 3.2.2), what an update learns from ( $d$ , Section 3.2.3), and how the update is made ( $u$ , Section 3.2.4). Section 3.2.5 then turns to how whole experimental loops are run, which can change several of these decisions at once, and Table 10 summarizes representative systems. A changed procedure is not yet an improved one: improvement requires that a learner guided by the revised procedure learn better than a matched learner guided by the earlier one (Section 2.2; Figure 8).

## 3.2.1 Choosing What to Practice

Once a recovery removes a coarse grasp failure, the remaining errors may concentrate on subtle contact events. More demonstrations of the old kind may then be less useful than tactile checks, a finer reward, or a new rule for editing skills. Better execution has changed the learning problem, and the system now has to find the experience that teaches what is still missing. The most useful experience for an improved policy need not come from the distribution that served its predecessor, and choosing it involves three decisions: which tasks or states to practice, what supervision an attempt needs, and when collection is safe. Active learning (Cohn et al., 1996) selects observations for their expected contribution to learning; in robotics, however, selecting an observation often means choosing a behavior, task, environment, or trajectory that must actually be executed. SAC-X (Riedmiller et al., 2018) schedules auxiliary behaviors that help the robot reach sparse rewards; automatic domain randomization (OpenAI et al., 2019) widens the range of simulated conditions as the policy becomes competent; and Enhanced POET (Wang et al., 2020) co-evolves environments together with the policies that solve them. In each case, what the learner can already do helps determine what it encounters next.

At the task level, the frontier worth targeting is learnable challenge: tasks hard enough to teach something and easy enough to be learned. Methods based on intrinsic motivation (Oudeyer et al., 2007), automatic curriculum learning (Portelas et al., 2020), and autotelic agents that set their own goals (Colas et al., 2022) track learning progress across goals or environment parameters. Systems built on language models enlarge the space of candidate lessons by generating tasks and environments, as in GenSim (Wang et al., 2024b) and RoboGen (Wang et al., 2024d); OMNI (Zhang et al., 2024) combines estimated learning progress with a model of what humans find interesting, while Eurekaverse (Liang et al., 2025) generates environments that become progressively more challenging while remaining diverse and learnable. AutoRT (Ahn et al., 2024) proposes tasks with language models, filters them against a written robot constitution, and over seven months collected about 77,000 episodes on 53 robots. Generation supplies possibilities, but a signal that depends on the learner’s current capability is still needed to decide which possibility should become the next lesson.

FIND (Fang et al., 2026) supplies such a signal on hardware: a VLM agent selects feasible tasks with low recent success and judges each attempt by comparing observations before and after execution, and residual RL on a frozen $\pi_{0.5}$ raises success on eight real tasks from 55% to 71.9%, while people still reset scenes when needed. InSight (Wang et al., 2026c) lets a VLM name the primitives a steerable VLA lacks for a new task, practices them with VLM-parameterized single-axis control, and retrains the VLA on successful segments; on a real xArm, twisting and pouring reach 92% and 96% against 32% and 16% for a zero-shot code-as-policy baseline with the same VLM (25 trials each). Agentic Skill Discovery (Zhao et al., 2024) has a language model propose the next task from its skill library and a pool of failed tasks, and keeps a skill only after a VLM confirms it; in simulation, RL reaches at least 90% success on 17 of 22 tabulated tasks. EmbodiedRSI (Song et al., 2026b) applies a related signal to an agent harness: it keeps competing code and skill hypotheses, selects the experiments expected to distinguish them, and learns which memories to keep according to their value for later improvement. It is evolved entirely in simulation (Table 11).

Goal exploration shows what such a signal should track. SAGG-RIAC (Baranes and Oudeyer, 2013) selects regions of the goal space where competence is changing, rather than repeatedly sampling the goals with the lowest success. Its experiments on learning inverse models move practice away from both mastered and unreachable regions. Failure prediction and learning-progress estimation answer different questions: where is the policy weak, and where is another attempt likely to change what it can do? ALAN (Mendonca et al., 2023) applies a related signal on hardware, exploring two real-world play-kitchen setups by favoring changes it can cause to objects and its uncertainty about predicted change. Practice Makes Perfect (Kumar et al., 2024) makes the choice explicit for parameterized skills. It estimates each skill’s competence, extrapolates the gain from another attempt, and weighs that gain by planned task success. Over one to three hours of reset-free practice on a Spot arm, the fraction of evaluation tasks solved rises from 0.0 to 0.8 in one environment and from 0.53 to 0.93 in the other.

At the level of states and datasets, failure becomes useful when it can be converted into appropriate supervision, and the clearest comparisons fix the demonstration budget while varying where it is spent. A criticality model trained on policy outcomes (He et al., 2026a) can bias collection toward scenarios that are likely to end in failure, such as hard initial states. The method uses importance-sampled experience for RL and criticality-selected expert demonstrations for imitation learning. In its two multi-round simulated runs the predictor is retrained on the current policy’s rollouts each round. Its evaluations span simulated locomotion and manipulation, VLA benchmarks, and a physical banana-placement task. A high criticality score still leaves open whether a state calls for autonomous exploration, expert correction, or avoidance. A world model can rank the gaps instead: RoboCoach (Liu et al., 2026d) imagines where each skill expert first times out and requests demonstrations for the highest-ranked subtask–expert pairs, and with matched demonstration budgets success rises from 13.3% to 75.0% on a Franka and from 40.0% to 83.8% on an AgileX platform, against 30.0% and 47.5% for uniform collection into one adapter. SAVE (Römer et al., 2026a) sends each expert demonstration to the task and initial state where an ensemble’s velocity fields disagree most, reaching 47% average success on three real Franka tasks at a fixed budget of 150 demonstrations, 8 to 25 percentage points above the baselines.

Failures can also be rebuilt or searched for in simulation. F4R (Yu et al., 2026b) rebuilds each diagnosed failure as a simulated scene for targeted practice and gains 18.75 percentage points out of distribution on four real tasks over a budget-matched targeted imitation baseline, and EmbodiRSI (Lang et al., 2026) builds task-specific simulations from deployment scenes for corrections and demonstrations aimed at current weaknesses (Table 11). SEES (Li et al., 2026l) restores, in simulation, the states before the sub-skill that most often stalls a long-horizon task and trains that skill family with PPO against an LLM-written success check, gaining over three rounds on RoboCasa365 (Appendix B). Q-DEG (Zhao et al., 2026a) searches over object placement, camera, and lighting for diverse simulated scenes in which a VLA sometimes fails and fine-tunes it on its own successes there, raising average success on held-out adversarial LIBERO-Spatial scenes from 67.2% to 80.9% for $\pi_{0.5}$ , with a small drop on the default scenes.

Supervision is the second decision: when experience must be acquired physically, selection must also determine when autonomy should yield to an expert. SAGE (Hu et al., 2026a) estimates short-horizon cost and uncertainty with an ensemble of world models and combines them into a “fear” signal, and world-model prediction error adjusts the threshold at which an expert takes over. The resulting interventions provide targeted data for imitation and risk-constrained learning. Most of its evidence comes from driving simulation and replay of Waymo driving data, while the hardware evaluation covers four controlled indoor settings for a ground robot with twenty trials per setting. The mechanism times expert takeover by predicted risk; long-term, unrestricted deployment remains untested. Human-in-the-loop deployment concentrates human attention in the same way. Sirius (Liu et al., 2023b) lets people intervene during deployment and learns from those interventions, whereas Fleet-DAgger (Hoque et al., 2022b) allocates a limited number of human supervisors across multiple robots. HIL-UMI (Han et al., 2026) carries the same idea into demonstrations collected with the handheld Universal Manipulation Interface (Chi et al., 2024): it queries the current policy without executing it, collects human actions where policy–human discrepancy indicates unfamiliar behavior, and separately refines a progress-based advantage estimator. It takes the robot out of demonstration collection, while human action supervision and final hardware evaluation remain necessary. Genie Centurion (Wang et al., 2025b) also updates the component that decides when to ask. Its task sentinel flags steps not completed in time, after which the robot rewinds for an operator’s correction, and the sentinel is fine-tuned together with the policy each round. Across four real tasks at matched trajectory counts, the final-round step-completion score is 0.93, against 0.53 for adversarial data collection and 0.38 for teleoperation.

Some desired experience cannot be demonstrated at first. GLIDE (Song et al., 2026c) converts anticipated and observed teleoperation failures into executable guardrails on commands and reuses the revised guardrails during both data collection and policy execution. Across three physical tasks, its ten-trial collection tests rise from zero or one successes without guardrails to seven to nine with the selected refined guardrails. Crossed policy experiments separate the contribution of guarded training data from that of guarded execution. Here, selection extends to constructing the conditions under which a demonstration can be produced at all.

Safety is the third decision. A learned runtime monitor (Liu et al., 2024) can request human supervision before a predicted failure, and safe online system identification (Zhang et al., 2025a) keeps informative trajectories within certified constraints. No single score resolves all three decisions: states with similar failure probabilities may represent a missing skill, an unreachable target, or an unsafe transition, so a useful rule for physical data acquisition must combine expected learning benefit with recoverability, the available source of supervision, and the costs of time, resets, wear, and risk.

## 3.2.2 Judging Outcomes

A policy that reaches new states also strains its supervision: a reward or evaluator learned on earlier behavior may not score those states correctly. The least invasive response is to update the judge and rescore existing data. PEBBLE (Lee et al., 2021) learns a reward model from preferences between pairs of behaviors (supplied by a scripted teacher in its quantitative experiments) and relabels the entire replay buffer whenever that model changes, so that off-policy learning in its simulated locomotion and manipulation tasks always sees one current interpretation of stored experience. RL-VLM-F (Wang et al., 2024c) keeps this preference-based loop but takes the labels from a vision-language model that compares pairs of images against a text goal, so that its simulated tasks need no human or scripted teacher.

FIERCE (Tan et al., 2026b) applies this response on hardware: it fits and calibrates an evaluator of progress and failure on local successes and failures. Each refinement round freezes a versioned snapshot of the evaluator, consistently relabels the replay buffer, updates the policy, and later refreshes the evaluator with newer rollouts. On physical peg-in-hole and cup-stacking tasks, the reported single-seed comparisons favor periodic refresh over a frozen task evaluator, although total acquisition cost is not equalized. Feedback thus stays stable during each policy update and can be revised between updates. RLER (Wu et al., 2026) refits a success-probability reward model on the policy’s own rollouts between rounds of offline RL; on two real Franka tasks, two rounds raise success from 64% to 84% and from 60% to 86% over the starting policy, 8 to 12 percentage points above keeping the same reward model fixed. EvoHIL (Zhang et al., 2026h) retrains its reward classifier during human-in-the-loop RL from operator-confirmed successes and deploys each version only after a held-out accuracy gate; under a lighting shift on six real tasks, success reaches 0.73 to 1.00 against 0.13 to 0.50 for HIL-SERL, in a comparison the authors describe as not resource-matched. VeriFine (Zhou et al., 2026b) revises the judge itself when policy gains plateau: an agent edits the judge’s rubric and asks human experts to settle disagreements, which on driving-reasoning data raises the judge’s correlation with human ratings from 0.55 to 0.85 and the policy’s reasoning score from 68.05 to 83.13 under matched training budgets.

A learned world model can also act as a judge, and it inherits the blind spots of its training data. On a physical arm, a scene-adapted Ctrl-World model fine-tuned on successes scored 90% of held-out failing sequences as success-like under a latent-distance diagnostic, and fine-tuning on execution-checked counterfactual failures lowered this to 33% (Xu et al., 2026c). Judges that only rank a frozen policy’s candidate actions at run time, without supervising an update, appear with action selection in Section 3.1.1.

A larger change is to revise the reward specification itself. Language models first entered this role as proxy reward functions. Kwon et al. (2023) prompt a language model with a task description and an episode outcome and use its answer as the reward in negotiation and matrix games. Language to Rewards (Yu et al., 2023) has a language model translate instructions into reward parameters that a model-predictive controller optimizes for a simulated quadruped, a simulated dexterous manipulator, and a real robot arm. Text2Reward (Xie et al., 2024) turns a natural-language goal and an abstract description of the environment into executable dense-reward code; after users inspect policy rollouts, their feedback can prompt another version of the code. Its main evaluations use simulated manipulation and locomotion, with policies trained in simulation demonstrated on two tasks with a Franka arm. Eureka (Ma et al., 2024a) removes the human from this editing loop by training policies under candidate rewards and returning task fitness, together with statistics of each reward component, to the language model. In Text2Reward and Eureka, the language model’s weights remain fixed; improvement comes from new context and externally supplied evaluation.

Later systems vary this loop mainly to cut its cost or to check candidates before training. STRIDE (Wu et al., 2025) refines humanoid-locomotion rewards from training statistics and reports a best success score about 2.5 times Eureka’s on random terrain in simulation, although the two methods use different language models. CARD (Sun et al., 2024) first checks whether a candidate reward ranks recorded successes above failures and skips RL training when it does not. It matches or exceeds expert rewards on 10 of 12 simulated tasks with about 14K tokens per task against about 663K for Eureka. MEMENTO (Sygkounas et al., 2026) evolves the evaluator before the policy, so that code-policy search in simulation solves Tower of Hanoi in 0.97 of episodes, whereas the same search under the unevolved evaluator solves none.

These loops also need a criterion that stays outside the quantity being optimized. A reward program can score demonstrations sensibly yet induce shortcut behavior during training; a quicker lift can coexist with excessive grip force, jerky motion, or a fragile final state. Text2Reward uses human interpretation as an anchor, while Eureka relies on a task-fitness function specified by researchers. Without such an independent reference, iterating on the reward can amplify a misspecification. Revising supervision has to ask two questions: does the new signal accelerate learning, and does the resulting behavior still satisfy the original objective?

Training conditions can be revised as well as rewards. DrEureka (Ma et al., 2024b) runs Eureka to write a reward, evaluates the resulting policy under perturbed simulator parameters to find the ranges in which it still performs well, and then asks a language model to choose a domain-randomization configuration (the ranges of physical parameters varied during simulated training) within those ranges. The resulting policies transfer to physical quadruped and dexterous-manipulation platforms without further real-world training. Here the policy probes its own later training distribution, but only in simulation; deployment experience does not feed back into that distribution. Broadening variation can increase robustness, yet overly wide ranges consume rollouts and can make the task unnecessarily hard to learn.

Physical verification can instead become memory for later judgments. REVOLVE (Liu et al., 2026b) stores cases in which a VLM’s judgment of an outcome disagrees with human verification and retrieves similar mismatches as context for future judgments. In parallel, it updates the policy from successful rollouts and from failure–correction data. Across four physical tasks, policy success rises over five iterations and judgment accuracy over four memory updates, supported by task-specific automated correction and reset routines. Because mismatch memory, policy data, and behavior change together, the study demonstrates a functioning dual loop without separating the contribution of each loop.

Retrieval can alter the effective procedure even when model weights stay fixed: ASPIRE uses earlier repairs to construct controllers, whereas REVOLVE uses earlier judgment errors to interpret later outcomes. FailBench (Navasardyan et al., 2026) measures how far such judges can be trusted: across 2,197 real and simulated manipulation attempts, the best of 13 VLM success detectors reaches 0.77 balanced accuracy, and none exceeds 0.60 on contact-intensive assembly. Every loop above that revises behavior against a learned judge is exposed to errors of this kind, so part of the success evaluation must stay outside the loop (Section 4.3.2).

## 3.2.3 Shaping Training Data

Once experience is collected and judged, the procedure must decide what the next update learns from: which retained data, corrections, demonstrations, or teacher outputs, and from which model. Several examples below run a fixed procedure and report a later-learning benefit; they appear here because they change where the procedure’s training data come from. Acquired competence first changes who can supply the next learner’s evidence. In RoboCat (Bousmalis et al., 2024) (Section 3.1.1), an adapted specialist collects data for the next generalist; the study demonstrates one completed cycle of this transfer. PLD (Xiao et al., 2026a) (Section 3.1.1) instead distills a specialist’s recoveries from the generalist’s own deployment states back into the generalist. The useful output in both cases is data that cover a task, a state, or a correction that the next learner would otherwise lack.

Acquired competence can also lower the cost of later learning in roles other than physical collection: VLAW (Guo et al., 2026) amplifies scarce real rollouts through a refreshed world model (below), ASPIRE (Lu et al., 2026a) avoids rediscovering a working program on a new robot API (Section 3.1.3), and CIDER (Li et al., 2026d) keeps earlier skills by reusing the accumulated policy as a teacher (Section 3.1.1). Verified solutions of coding agents can also become training data: EmbodiedSWE (Shen et al., 2026) expands one simulated solution into diverse demonstrations for a VLA, which then completes a long-horizon task on a real robot, and HexaAnything (Gao et al., 2026) trains a model on the verified execution traces of its own agent. Table 9 adds related cases from Sections 3.1 and 3.2 and lists, for each retained artifact, what is held fixed, the reported comparison and effort, and the scope of the result.

Fixed reward generator

A model that supplies training experience can itself be refreshed from new physical experience. VLAW (Guo et al., 2026) returns both successful and failed rollouts to world-model training, alongside retained data from the DROID dataset (Khazatsky et al., 2024), and fine-tunes a VLM-based reward model on first-round outcomes. The updated world model then generates closed-loop trajectories, and the VLA is trained on reward-filtered imagined successes together with real successes. Over two rounds and five real task categories, this full loop outperforms behavior cloning on filtered successes (Filtered BC) and DSRL while holding the number of physical rollouts fixed at fifty per category per round. Because model refresh, reward calibration, and synthetic data change together, the gain belongs to the pipeline as a whole.

Other systems refresh the practice model in the same way, and some isolate parts of the effect. World-VLA-Loop (Liu et al., 2026g) refits its video model and reward head on success and near-success rollouts of each new policy and restarts the policy from its fine-tuned starting point every round; on two real Franka tasks, two rounds raise success from 13.3% to 50.0% and from 26.7% to 53.3%, the second round adding 13.3 percentage points on both. WoVR (Jiang et al., 2026) refits its world model on rollouts of the already improved policy and, at the same 2,500-trajectory budget in LIBERO, beats adding base-policy data by 5.0 percentage points; its real-robot gains were obtained without this refresh. WorldSample (Xue et al., 2026b) refits a video model on accumulating real rollouts and admits its synthetic transitions into critic training by value balance and policy entropy, reaching 82% average success on five real tasks against 56% for HIL-SERL with the same demonstrations. MeRL (Li et al., 2026h) measures how far its updated world model diverges from stored real continuations and uses that error to set how much imagined data enters each update, gaining 3.5 percentage points on LIBERO-PRO over the same simulator updated without this trust layer. RIWANav (Xie et al., 2026a), which calls itself recursive self-improvement, lets the improved navigation policy choose which recorded action–video pairs refit its world model, adding 7.94 percentage points of success over a frozen world model on an offline urban-navigation benchmark.

Corrective actions can also come from outside the robot: dynamics models learned from human video propose them at failed states, and training on them raises success on seven real tasks across two robots from 40% to 81% (Chen et al., 2026b). A fixed multimodal agent can serve as that outside teacher in a recurring loop: Local Recovery Supervision (Zhang et al., 2026k) audits each round’s policy for unresolved failures, has the agent generate recovery demonstrations at those states, and trains the policy that drives the next round of collection. In a preliminary simulated study on LIBERO-Goal, recovery-augmented post-training of a $\pi_{0}$ checkpoint succeeds in 88 of 100 validation scenes, against 78 for continuing on the original data with the same additional optimization steps.

Selection also applies to experience that has already been retained, as when Memory Anchors (Du et al., 2026) (Section 3.1.1) enriches replay with selected old transitions. Criticality-guided sampling (Section 3.2.1) asks where new learning is most needed; Memory Anchors asks which earlier distinctions must remain visible while that learning occurs. Together they expose a trade-off between learning new behavior and keeping old behavior, one that the simpler instruction to “collect hard cases” hides.

Which gap matters also depends on the component that receives the data, as a world-model example shows. ConfAL-WM (Liu et al., 2026f) uses a confidence probe to allocate the post-training data budget of a world model across tasks, sampling episodes at random within each task, and gives larger training weight to frames and image patches where the model is least reliable. Under a fixed budget of selected data on the RoboTwin 2.0 benchmark (Chen et al., 2025c), confidence-guided selection improves scene consistency and trajectory metrics, and confidence weighting improves reconstruction, relative to alternative acquisition scores. Whether the same samples most improve control is a different question, since a transition that is informative about visual prediction may differ from one that supplies the policy with a missing action.

Rollouts can also decide which demonstrations to keep. Demo-SCORE (Chen et al., 2025a) trains a success classifier on the robot’s rollouts and removes demonstrations that resemble failures. On real ALOHA tasks, this improves success by about 30 percentage points on average over training on all demonstrations. CUPID (Agia et al., 2025) attributes closed-loop outcomes to individual demonstrations with influence functions and raises success on a real Franka task from 44% to 84% with 25 curation rollouts. Both curate once rather than as the policy changes. A model can also generate or vet its own training data. LACY (Hong et al., 2026b) uses one VLM to map language to actions, describe actions back, and judge their agreement, and filters its own generated data with that judge. RSI-Nav (Li et al., 2026f), described as data-centric recursive self-improvement, writes instructions for unlabeled trajectories, keeps those it can follow to the goal, and retrains before labeling the next shard. With about 2.5M generated samples, it raises simulated R2R-CE success from 52.9% to 68.6%. RE-0 (Zhang et al., 2026e) admits a stronger teacher’s code patches into a student’s training data only when paired rollouts from the same simulator snapshot show a gain, which lifts robosuite restacking from 4/50 to 31/50. Its ablations credit the admitted patches rather than the weighting of its distillation loss: on restacking, success-filtered teacher demonstrations stay at the base level, and on a lifting task the paired admission rule beats unfiltered and sign-only selection in every trainer seed.

These later-learning benefits do not require the robot to learn how to revise its learning procedure; often a fixed collection, filtering, or distillation recipe performs the transfer.

## 3.2.4 Constructing Updates

Once the data and supervision for an update are chosen, the update itself is usually still computed by a fixed rule. A further step is to change the procedure that produces the update. We use updater for a procedure that maps the learner’s current state, experience, and objective to an operation such as a parameter step, a loss function, an edit to a skill library, or a code patch. Section 2.3 reviews precedents that learn initializations, losses, and update rules, mostly in computation. Robot data have already trained part of an update mechanism. Meier et al. (2018) learn online a memory of learning rates that scales the gradient steps of inverse-dynamics models on a real Kuka LWR IV arm. They reuse it as the payload changes from none to light and heavy and reach lower prediction error early in each task. The memory’s own learning rule stays fixed, however, and the gain is measured in model error rather than in control. Here we follow updaters that act on robot learners and their artifacts. AdaRSI (Wang et al., 2026d), named for on-demand recursive self-improvement, decides where an update should go. A coding agent first refines its harness and fine-tunes the executor only on sub-steps that stay unreliable after retries from saved states. In RoboCasa365 simulation, this raises unseen composite-task success from 4.6% to 13.8%, slightly above fine-tuning every sub-step (12.6%) at 28% of its GPU time. Its agent also writes, from pilot tasks, an experience document and a training budget that guide later tasks.

PRACTICE (Bai et al., 2026) instantiates a related idea for an external skill library. Given trajectories and the current library, its learned editor can add, revise, merge, or remove skill entries, while a frozen executor uses the resulting library. The editor’s training progresses from successful oracle trajectories, through paired success and failure examples, to on-policy distillation from a stronger frozen teacher on the student’s own editing contexts. On the simulated EB-ALFRED household tasks from EmbodiedBench (Yang et al., 2025), the failure-aware stage reaches 45.3% average success, compared with 42.7% for a control trained only on successes, supporting the value of failures for training the editor. The broader gains across stages are harder to attribute, because the editor, its training data, and the accumulated library change together. What has improved is the editor; the executor, the external teacher, and the staged procedure that trains the editor all remain fixed.

When the value of a proposed change can only be established through robot interaction, constructing updates becomes inseparable from safe execution, resets, measurement, and recovery.

An updater can propose a new policy, loss, or training routine, but a physical system can judge that proposal only by running another experiment. Each candidate must begin from a known state, remain within safety limits, be deployed under recorded conditions, and receive an outcome that is sufficiently independent of the code being tested. Resets make successive trials comparable; verification supplies the selection signal; and logging connects a measured change to the hypothesis that produced it. These services belong to the learning loop itself.

ENPIRE (Xiao et al., 2026b) instantiates this loop for research on robot policies. In a human-guided construction stage, coding agents build task-specific services for safety, resets, and verification. The finalized services are then exposed as interfaces that cannot be modified, while coding agents revise the permitted training infrastructure or policy code, launch robot rollouts, inspect trajectories and videos, and retain or abandon hypotheses based on the resulting measurements. This covers more of the research process than executing a fixed fine-tuning script, while keeping the main outcome check outside the policy code that can be changed. Its autonomy still depends on the supplied task objective, demonstrations, hardware, and interface boundaries, and a misspecified verifier could consistently select the wrong revision.

ENPIRE studies two ways to extend a single experimental loop. Running more agent–robot pairs in parallel buys throughput at a resource cost; whether it also improves the updater is a separate question. In a separate transfer, agents distill their search on a pin-insertion task into a written summary and append it to the instructions for a GPU-insertion task, while removing prior trajectories, logs, and checkpoints. The result shows that procedural knowledge can be carried between related experiments; measuring how much that knowledge improves the later search would need a matched run without the summary.

Agents increasingly run the training pipeline itself. In simulation, a coding agent ran more than 70 quadruped-locomotion experiments in fourteen waves under human high-level direction (Khandelwal and Gupta, 2026). HARBOR (Li et al., 2026i) stages simulator setup, reward design, and tuning through gated sub-agents and appends distilled lessons to an experience memory, reaching 0.885 success on three-cube stacking in IsaacLab where Eureka and REvolve reach 0.0 under the same model and wall-clock budget. AURA (Zhu et al., 2025a) has language-model agents write staged curricula that specify rewards, domain randomization, and PPO settings, trains each stage in MJX simulation, and refines the next stage from its analysis, retrieving earlier curricula and feedback from a database; its hardware deployment on a custom humanoid is reported qualitatively. RoboClaw (Li et al., 2026e) pairs each forward manipulation with an inverse recovery behavior so that a dual-arm mobile manipulator can reset itself and keep collecting data, while one VLM agent coordinates collection, policy learning, and execution. It reports about 25% higher long-horizon success than its baselines, without stating whether the gain is absolute. It also reports that a manual baseline needs about 2.16 times its human time for the same amount of data. The RoboCoach system (Lei et al., 2026), unrelated to the world-model coach of Section 3.2.1, pairs a coding agent with a video-reading feedback agent: on RoboCoach-Bench, a set of 32 simulated tasks, the seven-model mean success reaches 0.730 against 0.602 for expert codebases, and on four real tasks in two laboratories the resulting policies reach 0.68 against 0.51 for matched human configurations.

Two studies measure what such loops still need. An agentic loop with evidence-gated promotion (Yu, 2026) cuts false promotions in LIBERO-10 simulation to 0.001 per run. Yet on its one measured lineage, its selections trail simply taking the final checkpoint (about 50–54% against 60% held-out success). Coding agents in RLE-Bench (Ma et al., 2026) likewise gain on the development evaluations they can query but lose verifier score on hidden robustness perturbations in simulation.

EndoRSI (Ng et al., 2026) brings a human-governed coding agent to an endoscopic robot: benchtop phantom trials rejected an insertion strategy that offline data had supported, and benchtop insertion success rose from 2/21 to 17/21 over four generations of revised policies.

Neighboring fields have automated other parts of the experimental loop. The AI Scientist (Lu et al., 2024) automates proposal, execution, analysis, and reporting in digital experiments. Robotic chemistry closes a physical loop within more structured apparatus: a mobile robotic chemist (Burger et al., 2020) and the A-Lab (Szymanski et al., 2023) select experiments, manipulate materials, and incorporate measured synthesis or characterization outcomes, while Coscientist (Boiko et al., 2023) adds planning and tool use by a language model. Across these settings, a reusable finding requires the candidate version, initial conditions, operating configuration, measurements, and failed trials to remain attributable.

Synthesis

Improving a learner and improving its learning procedure are distinct

• 

Practice should be selected for what it teaches. A reachable failure, a useful correction, and a learnable task differ, and the best target shifts as the policy improves.

• 

Judgment and updating can themselves change. Refreshed evaluators, retrieved guidance, and learned editors change later training; safety, reset, and verification services keep practice possible.

• 

The revised decision needs a downstream test. Component contrasts such as FIERCE’s refreshed evaluator, RLER’s refit reward model, and PRACTICE’s failure-aware editor indicate local procedure improvements; coupled pipeline gains improve whole loops without isolating the revision responsible.

• 

From procedure to improver. Whether an improver that learns from its own revision history produces better procedures on new physical failures is the first link of recursion (Section 3.3).

## 3.3 Improving the Improver

This section discusses mechanisms through which an improver could improve and the evidence offered for them. An improver’s knowledge may reside in weights, retrieved memory, code, or prompts (Section 2.2). After stating what would count as improvement, we group mechanisms by where that knowledge lives.

What would count as a better improver.

As Section 2.2 notes, successively better procedures can come from a fixed outer searcher. One example is a loop that its authors call policy-level recursive self-improvement: a criticality model updated from each round’s outcomes guides the next collection, but the update rule stays fixed (He et al., 2026a). An improver can also revise its own selection rules, such as how strict its admission gate is or which failures it debugs next; such a change alters the procedure that improves the system, yet it counts toward recursion only if the revised rules produce better later revisions than the earlier ones.

The missed grasp illustrates the difference between procedure improvement and recursion. Suppose a rollout reveals a visual signature of slip in an attempt that the reward program scored as a success. Using that signature to correct the reward and training a better policy under it would demonstrate procedure improvement. Here the reward program belongs to the learning procedure, the editor that writes it is the improver, and the records it keeps from such edits are its repair knowledge. The recursive route must also retain which failure distinction motivated the edit, under what conditions the edit was valid, and whether the downstream learner benefited. Those records could revise the reward editor’s parameters, retrieval memory, or rule-selection policy. The first link is present if the revised editor’s rewards for new failures teach matched learners better than the earlier editor’s rewards do. The second link is present if, given the same new repair histories, a revision of the editor proposed with what it has learned outperforms both the unrevised editor and a revision of the same editor proposed with that knowledge frozen.

The test leaves room for people and fixed machinery: human-specified goals, a fixed experiment runner, and an independently controlled safety boundary may remain in place. What must be traced is the source of the useful revision. If the gains came only from more trials, compute, or human assistance, a frozen-knowledge control given the same resources would match the updated version.

Against this standard, the physical systems covered by this review offer mechanisms and uncontrolled results for R1. We have not found sufficient evidence that freezes the acquired revision knowledge, matches the conditions of later learning, and isolates an R1 gain on new failures (Sections 4.1 and 4.4); the closest results, described next, come from simulation and do not yet meet all three conditions.

Learning from repair histories.

Several systems already keep records of earlier revisions and consult them when revising again, which places knowledge in the improver’s memory. AdaRSI’s (Wang et al., 2026d) coding agent writes, after pilot tasks, an experience document recording which sub-steps benefited from training and how much data sufficed, together with a training budget, and both guide later tasks (Section 3.2.4). HARBOR (Li et al., 2026i) distills recurring patterns from tuning rounds into an experience memory that later reward design and tuning retrieve (Section 3.2.5). Drawing on its accumulated experience in a separate run, it redesigns the reward for simulated three-cube stacking in 30 minutes rather than 4 hours, but on the same task and without a frozen-memory control; its ablation without experience removes templates, references, and heuristics, so the contribution of the lessons it accumulates on its own is not separated. AURA (Zhu et al., 2025a) stores earlier curricula, metrics, and human feedback in a database that conditions later curriculum, reward, and training-configuration generation (Section 3.2.5); without the database, the share of its generated curricula that launch training in simulation falls from 99% to 38% with GPT-4.1, but what AURA acquired itself is not separated from the human feedback. EmbodiedRSI (Song et al., 2026b) learns which memories to keep according to their value for later improvement (Section 3.2.1); on unseen RoboCasa365 composite tasks in simulation, this learned rule reaches 71.3% success against 61.7% for retrieval-based selection, a comparison of memory rules rather than of acquired knowledge. ENPIRE (Xiao et al., 2026b) carries a written summary of one search into the instructions for a related task (Section 3.2.5). In each case the improver’s knowledge changes; what remains to be shown is that this change, rather than added trials, compute, or human input, makes the next procedure better.

Two kinds of reuse sit close to R1 without reaching it. ASPIRE (Lu et al., 2026a) retrieves guidance from earlier program repairs, and the code-transfer study (Kuroki and Tang, 2026) supplies optimized programs as references; both improve how a fixed coding model constructs later task controllers (Section 3.1.3), which is evidence about constructing behavior rather than about revising collection, supervision, or updates. LMPC (Liang et al., 2024) trains the model that writes reward code for a model-predictive controller on past teaching sessions, so that teaching needs fewer turns. In simulation, a successful session on unseen tasks takes 1.9 rather than 2.4 chat turns (Section 2.3). That reward code drives real-time control rather than an RL training run, and the recipe that trains the model stays fixed. HarnessPAI’s (Wang et al., 2026g) growing library of failure-to-repair notes plays the role of ASPIRE’s guidance for its program writer: in simulation, three LIBERO-Object tasks reach full success within two rounds with the library rather than several without it, but no frozen-library comparison on new failures separates what the writer has gained (Section 3.1.3).

Revising the tools that revise.

PhysEvo (Tian et al., 2026), which its authors describe as physical recursive self-improvement, keeps a single frozen model but lets a meta-agent revise the task agent’s tools and skills and also its own diagnostic tools. In simulation it reaches 62.0% on held-out RoboDojo layouts, an equal-weight average over five capability dimensions, against 47.2% computed from public evaluation records for the strongest published comparison; on a real arm it succeeds in 84% of 25 trials while skill revision continues. It is the clearest physical case of an improver that edits its own instruments; the comparison that would attribute part of the gain to those edits, the same meta-agent with its diagnostic tools frozen, remains to be run.

Admission rules are another instrument an improver uses. When an optimizer agent revises a robot agent’s harness in simulated LIBERO tasks, a champion–challenger rule avoids the degradation that accepting every revision causes (Wei et al., 2026b). RegenHarness (Wang et al., 2026b) forbids its updater to weaken its own acceptance rule. In both cases the designers set the admission rule, so these results show which instrument an improver should use rather than an improver that improves it. MEMENTO (Sygkounas et al., 2026) evolves its rollout evaluator once before policy search, and VeriFine (Zhou et al., 2026b) revises its judge’s rubric with human calibration (Section 3.2.2); both change an instrument through a fixed process, which places them at procedure improvement unless the process that revises the instrument itself improves. STOP (Zelikman et al., 2024) is the digital reference point for such self-application. It applies a language-model-driven improver program to itself; with GPT-4, the self-improved improver raises held-out performance over a few rounds and transfers to new tasks without further optimization, whereas weaker models degrade, and the authors note that the unchanged language model makes this less than full recursive self-improvement. SICA (Robeyns et al., 2025) removes the split between meta-agent and target agent: the best agent so far edits its own codebase, and in one fifteen-iteration run with Claude 3.5 Sonnet, peak accuracy on a fixed 50-question SWE-bench Verified subset rose from 17% to 53%, with the same benchmarks used for selection and for reporting. A fixed base agent that makes every edit is the control that would separate better task tools from a better editor. VERSE (Wang et al., 2026k) lets a harness optimizer revise its own prompts, skills, tools, and notes as well as the executor’s harness, with model weights fixed. In its controlled study on held-out SWE-rebench tasks (one run per condition), self-revision without execution-based checks produced no harness that beat the initial one on validation, whereas with such checks it reached 41.05% against 39.20% for a fixed optimizer and 39.81% for checks alone; revising the reviser helped only when its edits could be tested. Predict Before You Modify (Xie et al., 2026d) adds to a self-modifying agent an editable model that predicts the outcome of each proposed modification and is revised from earlier modifications and their observed results; its robotics domain is quadruped reward design in the Genesis simulator, and its control with the predictor frozen, run on coding tasks, has bootstrap intervals that include zero.

Carrying repair knowledge across tasks and robots.

R1 needs repair knowledge that transfers beyond the failures that produced it (Section 4.4), and the evidence so far concerns mostly task-level artifacts. ASPIRE carries guidance from simulated Franka repairs to a bimanual robot with a different API (Section 3.1.3), and ENPIRE’s written summary moves from pin insertion to GPU insertion on real robots without a matched control. A simulated study of 123 rounds of agentic skill discovery (Wang, 2026) shows how accumulated revisions can stall: deployed changes kept passing their tests while the target task never succeeded. MC-EvoBench (Song et al., 2026a) sets out the controls a transfer claim needs: the target world and inventory are reset, only a declared artifact carries over, and related experience is compared with no-prior, unrelated, and shuffled sources at matched environment steps. In Minecraft, over five seeds, a memory-based agent showed no stable transfer, and related experience lowered stone-tool stage success from 0.150 to 0.050. Applying the frozen-knowledge comparison of Section 4.4 across failure families or robots remains to be run.

## 3.4 Closing the Recursive Loop

R2 asks for the last step: the improver, once improved and using what it has acquired, must help produce a next version that is better than itself (Section 2.2). Digital systems supply the closest precedents, because executable tests make each candidate cheap to judge, while physical systems supply boundary cases; the gap between the two points to the paths ahead. In the physical systems covered by this review, we have not found a controlled comparison that isolates an R2 contribution; the closest controlled evidence comes from a simulated reward-design task in a digital system, discussed below.

Digital precedents.

Digital systems also separate applying a fixed update recipe from making the updater itself an object of search. Expert Iteration (Anthony et al., 2017) alternates tree search with training a network to imitate the search, then uses that network to guide the next search; STaR (Zelikman et al., 2022) repeatedly generates rationales, keeps those that lead to correct answers, and fine-tunes on them. Both repeat a fixed loop. Promptbreeder (Fernando et al., 2024) evolves both task prompts and the mutation prompts that change them. ADAS (Hu et al., 2025b) searches over agent designs written as code, and ALMA (Xiong et al., 2026) searches over memory designs written as code and finds designs that outperform human-crafted ones in four sequential decision-making domains. AlphaEvolve (Novikov et al., 2025) couples code proposals from language models to executable evaluators.

The Darwin Gödel Machine (DGM) (Zhang et al., 2026c), which comes closest to the second link, modifies its own agent code and keeps an archive of variants. In an ablation in which a fixed base agent performs every modification, gains taper off quickly, whereas letting improved agents modify themselves sustains improvement on its coding benchmarks. That ablation is the closest analogue of the second link that we found. It differs from the physical case in three ways: the improver and the task solver are the same agent; the benchmark that selects each modification also measures it, although the discovered improvements also transfer to a held-out benchmark and to other foundation models; and executable tests reject candidates cheaply.

Other digital systems extend the pattern. Gödel Agent (Yin et al., 2025) lets an agent rewrite the code that performs its own improvement (Section 2.3). SelfSearch (Yang et al., 2026c) has agents modify themselves using records of earlier self-modification episodes, without downstream reward during the search, and improves population-mean success over the initial agent in all six model–benchmark settings it reports. HyperAgents (Zhang et al., 2026d) make the meta agent itself editable and run the fixed-improver control. With the initial meta agent held fixed for the same number of iterations, the median test score in simulated quadruped reward design reaches 0.213 against 0.372 when the meta agent improves itself (five runs each). Hyperagents transferred to Olympiad-level math grading and run with their meta agent fixed yield an improvement of 0.630 where the initial agent yields none. In AIDE2 (Srikanth et al., 2026), a fixed outer-loop agent rewrites a research agent’s code, and the seven accepted improvements transfer to four held-out benchmarks. When the evolved agent instead drives the outer loop, its final grade is close to that of the human-designed driver (0.780 against 0.782 over three seeds), a result its authors call inconclusive.

Other routes make the improver a learned component. Randomly initialized learned optimizers can train one another and, once better, speed up their own training (Metz et al., 2021), and a self-referential weight matrix modifies its own weights, including the transformations that generate later modifications, although its authors leave a dedicated measurement of self-improvement to future work (Irie et al., 2022). MASS (Lee et al., 2026a) fine-tunes one model that proposes, executes, and judges multi-agent workflows on its own traces; when workflows from two generations are run by the same base executor, those of the newer generation win 64% of comparisons, although discovery still used each generation’s own executor and judge, and its robotics tasks are simulated. SpecRSI (Xu et al., 2026d) trains its improver on completed searches with the outer update rule fixed and runs the original and the updated improver on the same released artifact, where the updated improver yields a gain of 6.9 against 5.7 percentage points; some of its joint-update conditions have unequal training cost.

Two recent systems sharpen what an R2 test must measure. The Huxley-Gödel Machine (Wang et al., 2026f) reports that an agent’s own benchmark score only weakly predicts how good its self-modified descendants will be (weighted correlations of 0.27 to 0.44), whereas a score pooled over its descendants predicts it better (0.63 to 0.78). Selecting by that pooled score, with the starting agent and an 800-evaluation budget held fixed, its best agent reaches 56.7% on a 60-task SWE-bench Verified subset against 53.3% for DGM and 50.0% for SICA in single runs, and improves from 34.8% to 40.1% on 207 held-out SWE-bench Lite tasks. The Red Queen Gödel Machine (Iacob et al., 2026) lets learned evaluators evolve alongside the agents they score and promotes an evaluator only when it beats the incumbent on a fixed ground-truth anchor. Against frozen evaluators, in one run per arm, its coder passes 82.1% against 75.0% of 28 held-out tasks (a winner found with more search tokens); in a simulated Unitree Go2 reward-design task, its mean held-out jump height is 0.365 against 0.306 m over five runs per arm. Its comparison isolates evaluator co-evolution while the meta-agent is editable in both arms, so, unlike DGM and HyperAgents, neither the Huxley-Gödel Machine nor the Red Queen Gödel Machine compares improved agents modifying themselves with a fixed base agent making every modification at a matched budget.

Physical boundary cases.

Several robot systems, some evaluated only in simulation, are described by their authors as recursive self-improvement or recursive improvement: the criticality model’s policy-level RSI, EndoRSI, EmbodiRSI, Local Recovery Supervision, MobiAgent, RegenHarness, Embodied Turing Machines, RE-0, AdaRSI, PhysEvo, D-World, RIWANav, and RSI-Nav; EmbodiedRSI and RoboRSI use the term in their names. Table 11 lists what each changes, where it was tested, and where this review discusses it. Judged by the claims of Section 2.2, they revise policies, models, skills, data, or procedures, and several also accumulate knowledge that guides their next revision (Section 3.3). PhysEvo, whose meta-agent edits its own diagnostic tools, comes closest in mechanism (Section 3.3). Turning any of them into evidence for R2 would require the R2 comparison of Section 2.2, run on held-out failures with matched learners.

Paths toward physical RSI.

Four design requirements follow from the physical constraints discussed above. First, a protected success evaluation, with an outcome check that stays outside what the improver can change (Section 4.3.2). Second, staged evaluation: candidate revisions are screened in simulation or on stored traces, and hardware trials go to matched learners for the few that survive (Section 4.4). Third, repair records that carry their conditions of applicability, so that the improver can tell when an earlier lesson no longer holds (Section 4.4). Fourth, a budget that counts trials, resets, wear, and supervision, because revision histories accumulate slowly when each entry costs physical rounds (Section 2.4). A roadmap of autonomy levels (Duan et al., 2026) separates structural recursion, in which a revised improver, verifier, or successor generator is merely inherited, from effective recursion, in which it produces better later improvements. In its own scheme of levels, the roadmap places embodied intelligence at the frontier of choosing improvement strategies and acquiring experience, with deployment adaptation shown mainly in simulation. Reaching the top level of the concurrent scheme in Section 2.2 (Zhou et al., 2026a) would require comparisons such as those designed in Section 4.4.

Synthesis

Robots have the machinery of recursion, not yet controlled evidence

• 

The improver’s knowledge already changes. Repair notes, experience documents, and a meta-agent’s own diagnostic tools change what robot improvers know and use.

• 

No physical comparison yet isolates a recursive gain. The closest results, from HARBOR, AURA, and EmbodiedRSI, are uncontrolled and come from simulation.

• 

Digital systems show that the control can be run. The Darwin Gödel Machine and HyperAgents compare improved agents modifying themselves with a fixed base agent, where executable tests make each candidate cheap to judge; physical revision histories accumulate slowly because each entry costs physical rounds.

• 

From mechanism to test. The two-round test of Section 4.4 would isolate either link.

\physicalaisectionaccent

PAICopper

## 4 Challenges and Future Directions for Physical RSI

This section turns to what stands in the way of physical RSI. Section 4.1 asks what current evaluation cannot yet measure and how judges can stay aligned as behavior improves; Section 4.2 explains why observed gains saturate, regress, or fail to carry forward, and how retained improvements can survive system change and finite resources; and Section 4.3 asks how practice can stay safe across rounds. Section 4.4 sets out what stands between current results and verified recursion, from locating the bottleneck and choosing a usable experiment to learning a transferable repair principle and testing whether it makes improvement recursive. Table 12 lists, for each open problem, the conditions it serves, its central question, and a starting benchmark.

## 4.1 Measuring Improvement

Section 2.5 described the benchmarks and metrics in use; this subsection asks what they miss. Benchmarks test a system at one point in time, whereas self-improvement unfolds over rounds, so the test tasks must stay outside what the revising system can tune against. HarnessPAI reports 96.5% on LIBERO-PRO evaluation seeds that include its development seeds, and RoboRSI improves online on its evaluation tasks while its baselines do not. In RLE-Bench (Ma et al., 2026), coding agents improve on the development evaluations they can query but lose 19 to 41 points of verifier score, out of 100, on hidden robustness perturbations in simulation. Held-out seeds, tasks, and perturbations that the revising system never queries are part of a protected success evaluation. A held-out set also loses its validity when its results are reused repeatedly to select candidates, because the selection can overfit the held-out set itself (Dwork et al., 2015).

Comparisons across rounds are rarer than single-round gains. Behavioral improvement is widely established (Tables 6–8), but later-learning and procedure claims need matched learners (Section 2.2). Few studies supply them. PLD does so for the training value of collected data on hardware, and FIERCE, RLER, WoVR, SAVE, and the demonstration-requesting RoboCoach do so for single procedure components. Integrated pipelines such as VLAW and the RoboCoach system (Lei et al., 2026), whose coding agent changes architecture, data augmentation, optimization, and validation code together, improve as a whole, and most results cover only the few rounds reported. For recursion, three judgments must be kept apart: a component of the procedure changes; the improver’s knowledge or tools change, as in AdaRSI, HARBOR, AURA, or PhysEvo; and a recursive gain is isolated by a frozen-knowledge or fixed-improver control with matched learners and budgets. The physical systems reviewed here reach the second judgment at most, while digital systems such as the Darwin Gödel Machine and HyperAgents run fixed-improver controls with executable tests or in simulation rather than through physical trials. Table 13 summarizes where the reviewed studies stand on each claim.

Table 13: Where the reviewed studies stand on each claim of Section 2.2. Systems are representative and follow the assessments in the sections that discuss them; those without an icon are digital.

Claim

Strongest support found

Representative systems

R2
