When retained knowledge enters a decision rather than weights, the corresponding measure is a component test that holds the decision-maker fixed and varies only the retained resource. ASPIRE (Lu et al., 2026a) compares debugging a real robot’s controller with and without transferred repair guidance, although whether starting programs and debugging budgets were matched is not stated; REVOLVE’s (Liu et al., 2026b) memory instead enters outcome judgments, so its test would compare judgments of the same observations under old and updated memory, a pathway that its reported judgment–policy trajectories do not isolate.

Even a well-designed comparison establishes improvement only relative to the success judgments made by a person, sensor, or model. Hardware studies differ in whether that channel is independent of the signal used for learning. RoboCat (Bousmalis et al., 2024) uses learned reward models for autonomous success detection and relabeling, but counts final evaluation outcomes manually. In SILVR’s real-robot experiments (Luo et al., 2026a), human judgments supply both the filtering signal and the evaluation outcome. Q-Planning (Giridhar et al., 2026) goes further: a human-provided label supplies the terminal reward on the real robot, and its per-iteration success rates are computed from the same collection episodes. Each protocol supports a different claim. A shared judge can measure improvement against its own criterion but cannot reveal a systematic error in that criterion; an independent outcome audit tests that additional link. The same caution applies to an agent’s own report of completion: in the GPT-6-Astra evaluation (Cai et al., 2026), self-reported progress diverged from the actual completion conditions on simulated tasks, and its proposed benchmark keeps judging the trajectory after the agent declares that it is done.

Tables 6–10 separate execution from outcome judgment for this reason. Final scorers, or their separation from training, remain unclear for SOAR, SI-EFM, RAPolicy, SARM2, VERITAS’s hardware tests, CIDER, RISE, PlayWorld, HIL-SERL, PLD’s hardware tests, and VLAW. SI-EFM’s collection labels and VLAW’s human filter audit do not establish independent final checks. DexTouch-WM specifies five human raters for its model-evaluation comparison; their role in the later mixed-data policy test is not separately stated.

Success-evaluation details are also unresolved for Robo-Cortex, Zeva, ReSYNC, ASPIRE, Uni-Skill, Criticality model, and GLIDE. REVOLVE provides human-referenced judgment accuracy without specifying the final policy judge. FIERCE reports independent terminal checks but insufficient detail to assess separation. These are reporting gaps, not evidence of incorrect judgments.

A success rate should be read with the number of trials and independent training runs behind it. A few trials leave a wide interval even at perfect success, and trials that share scenes, resets, checkpoints, or training runs are less independent than a binomial calculation assumes. Reports should therefore state trial and run counts, how tasks, initial states, and checkpoints were selected, and whether judges agreed or were blinded (Kress-Gazit et al., 2024). Differences between methods should carry their own uncertainty. Sequential designs lower the cost: AnyRank (Badithela et al., 2026) assigns more trials to policies that are hard to separate and needs up to 50% fewer hardware evaluations than earlier rigorous rankings. RoboArena (Atreya et al., 2025) instead ranks policies from double-blind pairwise comparisons crowd-sourced across seven institutions on the DROID platform, and AutoEval (Zhou et al., 2025d) supplies automatic success detection and scene resets so that real-world evaluation runs with little human involvement.

A cost comparison is meaningful only once the outcome and its uncertainty are specified. Cost accounting should separate setup and signal construction, acquisition of the revision, downstream training and evaluation, deployment, and later maintenance. ASPIRE reports model tokens to the first working program or to budget exhaustion, a measure that excludes robot time and total system cost. VLAW matches 50 physical rollouts per task category per round against its downstream baselines, while also fitting models and generating synthetic data. FIERCE reports 300 refinement rollouts per condition on each real task and explicitly excludes initialization, evaluator adaptation, calibration, evaluation, resets, and interventions from that count. These scopes answer useful but different questions, and ranking costs across studies would mix them. Table 9 preserves the reported scope; a complete comparison of the cost to reach a target would additionally count robot episodes and hours, resets and human time, compute or tokens, failed runs, and revalidation after later updates. Elapsed time belongs in this count too, because simulated benchmarks can pause while a model deliberates: in the GPT-6-Astra evaluation (Cai et al., 2026), the standard RoboDojo budgets correspond to 8–76 s of commanded motion against an average of 55 minutes of computation, so a larger step budget raises simulated success at no cost in elapsed time. On a real Unitree G1 humanoid, about 90% of each run went to deliberation and one manipulation trial took over an hour, and protective stops and actuator overheating ended trials on other robots. Its proposed benchmark therefore charges deliberation on a world clock that keeps running.

Keeping judges aligned.

Measurement inside the learning loop raises a related problem: an experiment becomes misleading if the supervision used to judge it no longer resolves the failures produced by the improved policy. The measurement of the task may then need to evolve without silently changing the task itself. An early grasping policy may mostly miss the object, so a coarse lift detector is informative. A stronger policy may lift the object faster while squeezing hard enough to damage it. Adding force-sensitive evidence can refine the measurement of the original intent (a secure lift without damage), whereas redefining success to favor speed despite damage changes the intended outcome. An evaluator update should state which task requirements remain unchanged and which previously missed failure modes it now detects.

Because behavior shifts toward subtler failures, an evaluator refreshed from examples it selected itself can reproduce its own blind spots, and one refreshed only on recent failures can forget distinctions needed for older behaviors. The supervision loop needs anchors that stay fixed across evaluator versions: outcome-linked examples spanning old and new failure modes, selectively requested physical or human judgments, and a protected success evaluation for disputed cases (Section 4.3.2). In the freeze–test–refresh–relabel sequence of Section 4.2, these anchors serve as the test. Versioning must connect each score to the evaluator, evidence, and task definition that produced it.

A benchmark for evaluator refresh should therefore separate better measurement from better supervision. With the learner, data, and update budget fixed, it varies only the evaluator and its refresh rule, tests judgments on independently selected old and new outcomes, and then trains the same learner from each version’s labels and evaluates the policies through protected physical outcomes. An evaluator counts as progress only if it preserves the intended ordering of outcomes across rounds and helps produce a better policy; fitting its refreshed labels more accurately is not enough.

Synthesis

Different claims need different comparisons

• 

Recursion is the next claim to test. Beyond a frozen-knowledge or fixed-improver control, a recursive claim needs independent success evaluation, evidence of durability, and an account of total cost.

• 

Success evaluation and cost qualify every claim. Who judges success, how many independent runs stand behind a rate, and what was left out of the cost count determine how far a result generalizes.

• 

Open question: a shared reporting standard. The reviewed hardware studies report success evaluation, independent runs, and cost accounting in different ways. Agreeing on a minimal standard built from the reporting items of Section 4.1 is among the most immediately achievable steps for the field, and it would make self-improvement results comparable across laboratories.

## 4.2 Accumulating Improvement

The gains reviewed in Section 3 are often real but local. Consider the missed grasp once more. After the robot learns to grasp reliably, its new successes may teach little that it does not already know, and its contact model may not cover the faster motions it now makes. A repaired grasp may expose a weakness in the controller that executes it. A growing library of grasp skills may become hard to search, and maintaining all of this machinery may cost more than it saves. These failures map onto the conditions of Section 2.2. Narrowing data strains informative feedback, outgrown models and rewards reflect a lack of robustness to distribution shift, and a moving bottleneck is a problem of usability. Accumulation strains both usability and robustness to distribution shift, and infrastructure cost bears on continued practice. Table 14 lists the comparisons that bear most directly on each condition. Carrying a benefit forward means that it remains useful for later behavior or learning; performance need not grow at an accelerating rate. Because recursion depends on gains that carry into the next round, each of these stalls is also an obstacle to RSI.

When data stop teaching.

When autonomous imitation keeps only successful rollouts (filtered behavior cloning), the next dataset increasingly reflects behavior that the current policy can already produce. A higher success rate can then raise the yield of accepted trajectories while each new trajectory teaches less. The plateaus reported by Mirchandani et al. (2025) are consistent with this pattern. PLD (Xiao et al., 2026a) shows the complementary case: collectors with equal success can differ in training value.

When success and training value diverge, choosing what to practice next (a curriculum) must answer two questions. The first is where another trial is informative, which failure prediction and competence progress answer differently. The second is what supervision the selected state needs. If an adequate recovery already lies within the range of behavior the learner can produce, autonomous practice may expose it. If no useful action is available, repeated failure adds little, and an expert correction, a residual controller, or a new skill may be required.

The choice must also move with the policy. A state that once exposed a useful error can become routine after correction, while better recovery and reset mechanisms can make previously costly states reachable enough to practice. Conversely, an irreversible or unreliable reset can make a theoretically informative failure a poor use of a physical budget. Collection, correction, and recovery should be reassessed together, judged by the downstream learner’s gain and the physical cost.

When models and rewards fall behind.

A moving curriculum changes the distribution of situations in which the next policy acts, and predictors and evaluators fitted to earlier rounds can lose their footing. Two distinct forms of drift result. First, a world model may have little support (few comparable training examples) for the improved policy’s actions and contacts, such as the faster grasping motions it now makes, so optimization can favor trajectories that look successful only because the prediction is wrong. Second, an evaluator trained on earlier behavior may remain accurate on old failures yet miss the subtler ones produced by a stronger policy. Agreement between prediction and evaluation is weak evidence when both are computed from the same imagined trajectory and share the same blind spot about the physical world.

Existing methods respond at three different points. WISE (Zhang et al., 2026a) restricts imagination to selected states and short horizons, HaWMPO (Chen et al., 2026j) downweights rewards from chunks it scores as hallucinated, and VLAW (Guo et al., 2026) refits the world model on new physical rollouts. Restriction and downweighting protect an update within the model’s current support; refreshing attempts to enlarge that support. The trade-off is temporal: a conservative imagination region can be reliable now yet exclude the transitions required for the next improvement, whereas expanding it consumes physical trials and helps only if collection reaches the new policy’s relevant errors.

In PlayWorld (Yin et al., 2026b), prolonged fine-tuning inside the model can keep improving the imagined reward while reducing success on hardware, as the policy specializes to small prediction mismatches, so even a comparatively accurate model remains open to exploitation by optimization. Fresh physical rollouts act as an external anchor for deciding when to stop, refresh, or distrust imagination. Studies of generative models trained repeatedly on their own outputs provide an adjacent warning: without enough real data, such loops can progressively lose the tails of the data distribution (Shumailov et al., 2024) or lose diversity (Alemohammad et al., 2024), a coverage failure that any mixture of real and imagined data must monitor.

Evaluator updates require a complementary timing rule. Combining FIERCE’s per-round refresh, which already freezes a versioned evaluator and relabels replay (Tan et al., 2026b), with PEBBLE’s relabeling (Lee et al., 2021) suggests a freeze–test–refresh–relabel sequence: keep feedback stable during an update, test it against current outcomes, revise it when behavior shifts, and make retained data consistent with the new version.

When bottlenecks move.

Every repair, including the repairs of prediction and feedback just described, also changes what the next component receives. A bottleneck moves when a previously adequate recipient becomes the dominant source of end-to-end error. Two ablations expose such a recipient: in SILVR’s simulated ablation (Luo et al., 2026a), a frozen action translator limits what an updated video planner gains, and in SHAPER (Wang et al., 2026e), skill guidance and the harness that delivers it have non-additive effects. Neither study follows the bottleneck across rounds, so the consequences below remain hypotheses that a multi-round test would need to check.

When an execution module admits a wider range of behavior, the predictor and evaluator should be retested on the newly reached states. A runtime that rescues failures may hide from the learner the raw mistakes that motivated the rescue, unless trajectories from before the recovery and the corrections themselves are retained. A growing skill set forces the harness to decide which repair applies and which evidence should be preserved. A simulated study of 123 rounds of agentic skill discovery (Wang, 2026) shows the resulting stall: changes kept passing their tests, yet the target task never succeeded, because perception could not represent the needed relation, learning concentrated on the first step of long skill chains, and the agent optimized the evaluator’s errors. A convincing test should evaluate the full pipeline on the distribution reached after the repair, ablate the receiving component, and inspect the next learning update, in addition to rechecking the repaired component.

When knowledge is lost or misused.

As repairs accumulate, the usability problem becomes one of retention and retrieval. Retention asks whether an earlier behavior remains available after the next update; retrieval asks whether the right behavior or record is selected under the present conditions. A system can satisfy one without the other. An old skill may survive in the weights yet be routed incorrectly, while a relevant trajectory may be retrieved but decoded through an action interface whose coordinates have changed. Lasting improvement must preserve content together with the distinctions, interfaces, and applicability conditions that make it usable.

Existing methods protect different parts of this path. Three methods rehearse earlier behavior: Memory Anchors (Du et al., 2026) through targeted replay, CIDER (Li et al., 2026d) through distillation, and Dream to Remember (Tharwat et al., 2026) inside a frozen world model. Pretraining changes how much protection is needed. On LIBERO suites in simulation, pretrained VLAs forget far less than small policies trained from scratch, simple experience replay sometimes removes forgetting, and skills that appear forgotten recover quickly with finetuning (Liu et al., 2026c). Interpolating finetuned weights with the pretrained ones retains generalist abilities while learning a new task, in simulated and real experiments (Yadav et al., 2026). Dream to Remember also learns new tasks in imagination, but only where that model is accurate enough. ACCRUE (Kim et al., 2026) instead carries lightweight actor–critic networks across a task sequence, cutting real-robot training time for later tasks by 31.0–68.5%, and FAN (Hong et al., 2026a) keeps the numerical meaning of retained actions fixed. These mechanisms complement one another: better rehearsal cannot repair coordinate drift, and stable coordinates do not decide which memory or skill applies. Their evaluations also use bounded task streams, so retrieval from an indefinitely growing repertoire is still to be tested.

External memories make that final problem explicit. Robo-Cortex (Chan et al., 2026) consolidates episode histories into retrievable principles and heuristics, whereas AllDayNav (Yin et al., 2026a) stores temporal context and can replace or extend visual memories as navigation experience accumulates. Their reported system-level gains support the usefulness of maintained memory; a general rule for detecting when a once-valid record has become stale has yet to be developed.

Evaluation should expose each burden separately. The task suites of Section 2.5 provide breadth, but each still needs a continual-learning protocol on top. After every update, the informative record is a matrix: performance on the new task, on earlier tasks, on deliberately conflicting states, and under shifted conditions in which stored knowledge may no longer apply. The matrix separates forgetting, retrieval error, and stale reuse, which a single final success rate would collapse together.

Surviving system changes.

Repair knowledge remains useful only while its applicability conditions hold. A system revision can break these conditions at several interfaces: a sensor or calibration change alters observations, a controller or API change alters action semantics, and a new gripper changes contact dynamics and reachable effects. Whether the change is labeled “hardware” or “software” matters less than which relation the retained artifact assumed. Replacing a gripper may invalidate a force threshold or joint-space recovery while preserving the higher-level strategy of releasing, retreating, and regrasping; renaming an API call may break code while leaving the intended behavior realizable through a new wrapper.

Maintenance has to operate on artifact-specific conditions. Policy weights depend on observation and action representations and on dynamics, and a learned evaluator depends on sensing and calibration. A skill depends on its preconditions, effects, and available controller, and code depends on APIs, schemas, timing, and permissions. A gate, shield, or monitor depends on the policies and dynamics against which it was validated. A single timestamp or confidence score for a whole memory cannot show which assumption has become stale. Each retained change should instead state its validity conditions: its required inputs, action meanings, physical envelope, software dependencies, and the evidence under which it last worked.

Those conditions support four different responses. An artifact can be reused when its conditions still match, adapted when the task meaning is preserved but an interface can be translated, selectively revalidated when support is uncertain, or retired and rolled back when a required effect is no longer realizable. The distinction also guards against a misleading transfer claim. If a semantic recovery strategy succeeds on a new gripper only after a newly engineered adapter reconstructs its actions, the result tests the strategy and adapter together; the original commands were not what transferred. Conversely, rejecting the strategy because its old joint commands fail would overlook knowledge that remains valid at a higher level of abstraction.

A benchmark could introduce declared changes to sensing, embodiment, controller timing, APIs, and memory schemas, followed by gradual drift in calibration or wear, and compare unconditional reuse, relearning from scratch, and condition-aware adaptation or revalidation. Freezing the retained artifact while varying the adapter, and then the reverse, would separate semantic transfer from interface repair; the evaluation asks whether the system identifies the invalidated condition and restores held-out behavior without unsafe execution.

When costs outrun gains.

Building a world model, evaluator, reset routine, or repair library consumes physical trials, engineering, supervision, and compute; reusing it can save later trials but also requires storage, calibration, and validation. Such an investment is worthwhile only if the artifact remains useful long enough to repay its cost. If setup costs $C_{0}$ and reuse $i$ saves $s_{i}$ while incurring maintenance cost $m_{i}$ , all counted in one designated resource such as robot hours, the investment pays back only when $\sum_{i}(s_{i}-m_{i})>C_{0}$ before a change in task, scene, or robot makes it obsolete (the payback period). When several resources are involved, the criterion applies to each separately rather than to their sum. This is a design criterion that the surveyed studies do not yet estimate; reporting it alongside a comparison would be a natural next measurement.

ENPIRE (Xiao et al., 2026b) provides a concrete but bounded example: its safety, reset, and verification interfaces are built once and reused during later policy search. In its fleet experiments, scaling from one to eight agent–robot pairs reduces the time to reach the target from roughly five to two hours on Push-T and from more than 1.5 hours to about 40 minutes on pin insertion. The same scaling study reports lower mean utilization per robot and faster-than-linear growth in token consumption at eight agents; neither a cost-optimal fleet size nor the rate at which costs are amortized across tasks has been measured yet.

The appropriate repair depends on both expected reuse and the risk of invalidation. A stable embodiment and a recurring family of tasks can justify expensive retraining or reusable infrastructure, whereas an imminent change in hardware or control interface favors a smaller, reversible repair whose assumptions are easier to revalidate. The resources involved are not interchangeable: more compute cannot undo hardware wear, a faster fleet cannot replace a success evaluation that no longer detects damage, and recovery can preserve access to trials without making those trials free. Comparisons should hold the target capability fixed and extend the cost accounting of Section 4.1 with robot utilization, the period over which the acquired capability is reused, and retained performance after later updates.

Budgeting physical resources.

Beyond explaining why costs can outrun gains, the open problem is how to allocate robot time, human attention, computation, reset effort, and hardware wear over a deployment horizon. Simulation can replace some hardware interaction but creates model-fitting and physical-validation costs, and safety exposure should remain a constraint rather than be traded against savings in time or computation.

An allocation decision should combine expected downstream learning, frequency of reuse, maintenance and revalidation costs, and the probability that a task or system revision invalidates the artifact. It should also preserve option value: a reset capability, spare hardware capacity, or a human escalation path can be worthwhile because it keeps a future informative trial possible, even when it does not immediately raise task success. Three efficiency indicators can complement the causal tests: whether each round yields a gain, whether the gain arrives faster than human-engineered iteration, and whether the cost of each further gain, counted in robot time, attention, and wear, falls. A falling cost can also come from reused infrastructure or easier tasks, so it bears on the second recursive link only after the controlled comparison of Section 4.4 has established that link.

A deployment-horizon benchmark could present a sequence of tasks, failures, maintenance events, and system changes under finite resource capacities, let a method choose at each stage whether to collect, simulate, request supervision, build reusable infrastructure, revalidate an artifact, or retire it, and compare a horizon-aware allocator with myopic and fixed-budget schedules. Because no universal conversion rate relates robot time, human time, compute, and wear, results should report their trade-offs and their sensitivity to the assumed horizon rather than a single cost.

Synthesis

Diagnose the next broken connection, not only the current score

• 

Each fix changes the next round’s problem. Once kept data reflect what the policy already does, successes stop teaching and the next signal must distinguish a remaining failure; a repair changes what the next component receives, so the receiver needs retesting too.

• 

Models and judges fall behind. Freeze feedback during an update, then test, refresh, and relabel as behavior shifts.

• 

Infrastructure must repay its cost. Savings net of maintenance must exceed setup cost before the task, scene, or robot changes.

• 

Each failure appears in bounded tests. Table 14’s comparisons expose each failure within a few rounds, tasks, or configurations; none yet tracks interacting failures over a long deployment.

• 

Open question: can this diagnosis become part of learning? In the reviewed studies, researchers diagnose these failures after the fact. A direct test would inject one failure of each kind during a long deployment and score whether the system identifies it and schedules the right next experiment (Section 4.4).

## 4.3 Keeping Improvement Safe

For a robot that improves itself, safety preserves continued practice, the opportunity on which another learning round depends: a robot that damages its gripper or the object while practicing loses not only that attempt but also the trials that would have followed. Each update can also invalidate safety machinery built for the earlier system: an updated controller may exceed the assumptions of a safety guard, and a revised reset routine may no longer return the robot to a safe starting state. A recursive system faces this at two levels, because a revised learning procedure can also change what its safeguards must cover. Surveys of safe reinforcement learning (García and Fernández, 2015) and of safe learning in control (Brunke et al., 2022) catalog safeguards for collection, judgment, update, and execution. Some mechanisms below have been tested in simulation, formally, or on hardware; others are design requirements inferred from the ways self-modification can fail.

## 4.3.1 Keeping Practice Possible

Resetting is itself a capability that determines which experiments remain possible, as Leave No Trace (Eysenbach et al., 2018) and ReLMM (Sun et al., 2022) show. Returning to an accessible starting state preserves the next experiment; a human reset preserves it too, but by moving part of the work outside the autonomous system. The resource that matters is safe access to an informative state, which a count of rollouts measures poorly.

Keeping practice available also means maintaining the interfaces that make another trial interpretable after wear, calibration drift, or software change.

Self-modification creates two windows of safety exposure. During improvement, harm can come from exploration, data collection, resets, and candidate testing before an update is accepted. After an update is accepted, the revised system can visit states or issue commands outside the assumptions of its existing monitors and recovery routines. The two windows can compound. A robot might first crush an object during exploration. A revised reset might then leave the object where the gripper cannot reach it, and an outcome detector that misses the damage can retain that transition as useful experience. Safety is a property of the whole loop of collection, judgment, update, and execution, not of the deployed policy alone.

Existing methods protect different parts of this loop. Safe exploration based on recoverability (Moldovan and Abbeel, 2012) formalizes safety as keeping, with a chosen probability, a route back to a home state. Constrained policy optimization instead keeps policy search near constraint satisfaction at every iteration (Achiam et al., 2017). Recovery RL (Thananjeyan et al., 2021) learns this judgment from data, in simulation and on a surgical robot: a safety critic estimates the chance of constraint violation, and a learned recovery policy takes over when that chance crosses a threshold. The Neural Simplex Architecture (Phan et al., 2020) switches to a baseline controller when a learned controller issues a potentially unsafe input, retrains the learned controller online, and returns control to it, in case studies on a ground rover and an artificial pancreas. Learning-based control (Brunke et al., 2022) instead constrains behavior relative to a model of the dynamics and its uncertainty, for example through control barrier functions (Ames et al., 2019) that keep the state in a certified safe set; such guarantees remain conditional on assumptions about state estimation, actuation, latency, and the operating envelope. Each notion covers part of physical safety. Task success establishes that an intended outcome occurred; failure detection covers only the failure modes that a detector represents; and constraint satisfaction is meaningful only relative to the specified constraint and model. A self-modifying system can pass one of these tests while failing another.

The scope of a proposed modification determines which assumptions must be revalidated. Policy learning changes which actions are taken, and model-based practice can misestimate contact. A revision of memory, runtime, or resets can alter recovery, and a revision of feedback can change which failures are visible. For each candidate change, a record of assumptions should note what changed, which safeguard depends on it, what was rechecked, and which physical hazards remain unresolved. Table 15 applies this analysis to the objects that the four uses of retained change can modify.

A recent survey of self-evolving humanoid robots (Nguyen et al., 2026) similarly organizes safety around admissible changes, verification gates, and boundaries for human oversight, proposed as design principles for later testing; a survey of safety in self-evolving language-model agents (Chen et al., 2026c) finds the evidence strongest for admission and local containment and weakest for repairing what later versions inherit. A runtime guard can reduce risk while one candidate executes, yet the update process is safe only if admitting an update also revalidates the guard’s assumptions after the change.

Table 15: How a revision can invalidate a safety assumption.

## 4.3.2 Protecting the Judge

A learning system can increase its recorded reward without improving the intended physical outcome. Several mechanisms produce this gap, and each needs its own defense. A misspecified objective rewards the wrong behavior; an ordinary evaluator error mismeasures an otherwise relevant outcome; specification gaming exploits an existing mismatch; and tampering changes the process that generates the learning signal. Amodei et al. (2016) separate problems such as side effects, reward hacking, and costly supervision, while Everitt et al. (2021) distinguish modifying the reward function from modifying its inputs. Moving the camera so that a slipping grasp is hidden affects the evaluator’s input, whereas editing the grasp-success checker affects the evaluator itself.

Even ordinary evaluator errors matter once judged outcomes determine which experience is kept. Mirchandani et al. (2025) identify accurate success detection as part of the burden of designing environments for autonomous robot data collection. A false positive can place a failed contact in a dataset filtered for successes, after which training can reproduce the pattern that was mislabeled as success. No deliberate manipulation is required: a detector error at judgment time becomes a data error at update time and can return as a behavioral error in the next round. Versioning the evaluator together with the data it labeled makes this propagation traceable, though finding the wrong labels still requires an independent check. In SEAR’s simulated self-exploration (Wang et al., 2026h), one agent run saved a success verifier that was wrong on 91% of the 699 later rollouts it declared successful, and the run ended at 4.6% success, against 15.5% and 23.6% for sibling runs without it.

Once feedback itself can be revised, testing a candidate evaluator only on outcomes or labels that it helped select becomes circular. Its admission test needs a protected success evaluation: a source of outcome judgments that the candidate update cannot also rewrite and that remains informative about the relevant physical failure. Depending on the hazard, this channel can combine held-out physical trials, write-protected raw measurements, dedicated instrumentation, and human review. ENPIRE’s verification services, which its coding agents cannot modify, are one physical instance. A report should state which failure modes the channel can reveal, which components it trusts, and which blind spots remain.

## 4.3.3 Admitting and Undoing Changes

An admission rule, or gate, turns protected evidence into a decision about whether a candidate update should run, trading the risk of accepting a harmful update against the cost of rejecting a useful one. In the notation of Section 2.2, a confidence-based gate admits a candidate learner $L^{\prime}$ only when a lower confidence bound on $J(L^{\prime})-J(L)$ is positive. Batch RL formalized such gates early: high-confidence policy improvement guarantees, at a chosen confidence level, that a returned policy performs above a chosen lower bound (Thomas et al., 2015), and SPIBB falls back on the baseline policy wherever the data leave the estimate uncertain (Laroche et al., 2019). In a constructed one-step pushing diagnostic, Ma and Wu (2026) hold the stream of candidate updates fixed and find that a gate based on a conservative range-based confidence bound admits none of them, whereas a paired-binomial gate admits 31.6% of them at 2,000 episodes per stage; in separate closed-loop runs, always adopting the replay-trained update yields better learning than either gated alternative. The evidence is analytical and synthetic; a hardware safety comparison has yet to be run. Its broader lesson is that admission should report both how well it controls errors and how many learning opportunities it preserves at a stated interaction budget.

Robot-agent studies show the effect of the gate itself. When an optimizer agent revises a robot agent’s harness, accepting every revision degrades it within a few rounds, whereas a champion–challenger rule raises held-out success on simulated LIBERO tasks from 51% to 67% with 100 rollouts per round (Wei et al., 2026b). In simulation, Robo-COP (Zhang et al., 2026i) reaches 73.8% when it adopts a fine-tuned policy only after verifying an improvement, against 65.8% for fine-tuning on a fixed schedule. A gate should also report what a candidate loses: a net gain can hide cases that the previous version solved, so a per-task regression check belongs beside the aggregate test. RegenHarness (Wang et al., 2026b) makes such a protocol explicit for a robot-agent harness: a candidate change to context rules, task templates, routing, or recovery policies is accepted only if it preserves safety and evidence contracts, passes designated regression checks, improves a predeclared metric, and receives release authorization. The updater cannot weaken its own acceptance rule. Its real quadruped deployment documents the runtime rather than measuring gains from revision.

Once a candidate is admitted, runtime mechanisms limit what each trial can do, and they intervene at different points. The shield of Alshiekh et al. (2018) corrects actions that would violate a property stated in temporal logic, a formal language for requirements over time such as “never enter this region”. The learned monitor of Liu et al. (2024) instead predicts states that are out of distribution or likely to fail and requests human oversight. The first is conditional on a specification and a model of the environment; the second is conditional on learned dynamics, failure labels, and the coverage of deployment. Neither protects against a hazard absent from its representation.

Other mechanisms move the check earlier or broaden the boundary around execution. VASO (Yang et al., 2026e) checks whole plans, model-checking skill-based plans against temporal specifications and using counterexamples to revise reusable skill contracts; GLIDE (Song et al., 2026c) checks single commands, filtering teleoperation and policy commands through task-specific executable guardrails; and PhyAgentOS (Liu et al., 2026h) wraps whole supervised sessions in compatibility checks before execution, verification, monitoring, and evidence collection. Safe online system identification (Zhang et al., 2025a) shows the value of stating assumptions precisely: its informative manipulator trajectories respect modeled input, state, and collision constraints, while its bounds on estimated parameters assume bounded sensor noise. Formal compliance, learned failure prediction, command filtering, and runtime mediation provide different kinds of evidence, and none translates directly into an unconditional probability of physical safety.

Systems that change their own software require a further boundary around authority. An updater should receive only the tool, file, network, and actuation permissions needed for the current experiment, while an independently controlled deployment service retains emergency stops, workspace limits, safety-critical calibration, and the authority to approve promotion. Each proposed change needs a provenance record linking the generating model, the prompt or memory used, the code diff, the data and evaluator versions, the test results, and the decision. A correct outcome does not show that each action rested on evidence gathered first; Lin et al. (2026) make this point for tool-using software agents by auditing trajectories against a provenance-bound evidence ledger. The checker can still err, but no single permission path can then silently rewrite both the candidate and every mechanism intended to assess it.

Promotion can then proceed from sandboxed tests of interfaces and logic to guarded physical trials within a declared operating envelope. Each stage should record which assumptions it tested and which physical unknowns remain; success in simulation alone cannot establish contact safety or hardware compatibility. Recovery also has to respect dependencies among components. A previous grasp policy may no longer work with a revised model, memory schema, or runtime, so rollback should restore a compatible versioned bundle rather than an isolated checkpoint. Even such a rollback restores only computational state. Trial limits and a stopping mechanism are still required before an update is admitted, because no rollback can undo a crushed object or a damaged gripper.

## 4.3.4 Placing Human Oversight

Gates and safeguards face a practical dilemma: continuous human monitoring limits scale, but removing supervision leaves unfamiliar failures without a reliable response. Human attention is most informative where automation lacks either a safe action or trustworthy evidence: before an irreversible contact, at a state the policy has not been trained on, when outcome signals disagree, or when a safety-relevant component is proposed for revision. These situations assign the human different roles: taking control prevents harm, a corrective action supplies a learning target, an audit adjudicates an outcome, and an approval authorizes a change. Recording these roles separately matters, because one intervention can otherwise be counted simultaneously as safety, as training data, and as evidence of autonomy. Interruptions can also bias what is learned: an agent rewarded for finishing a task may learn to avoid being stopped, and safe interruptibility requires that learning neither seek nor avoid interruptions, a property that off-policy learners such as Q-learning already have and Sarsa can be given (Orseau and Armstrong, 2016).

Timing determines whether an intervention supplies that information. HIL-SERL (Luo et al., 2025) uses demonstrations and human corrections during real-world RL, intervening often early in training and less as performance improves. ThriftyDAgger (Hoque et al., 2022a) requests help at states that are novel to a policy ensemble or risky under a learned estimate of task success, with thresholds set from an intervention budget. SAGE (Hu et al., 2026a) makes the threshold itself adaptive, so that predicted risk, uncertainty, and novelty decide when an expert policy takes over. These illustrate two allocation strategies, operator judgment and learned triage; neither is shown to detect every unsafe state. A late intervention may rescue the object without preventing a dangerous contact, whereas an overly conservative trigger can replace so much autonomy that the learner learns little about the limits of its competence.

A rescue also changes how the episode should be learned from. If an autonomous grasp starts to slip and a person then completes the lift, crediting the final success to the entire trajectory can reinforce the very action that required correction. TORL-VLA (Zheng et al., 2026a) addresses this case in real-robot experiments with an intervention-censored critic, which prevents success achieved after an intervention from being credited to the policy’s actions before the takeover. The broader requirement is a record of who was in control: each transition should identify who acted, why control changed, and which later outcomes may legitimately supervise the preceding decision.

Fewer takeovers can coexist with more oversight overall, because control effort may fall while learning effort (demonstrations, corrections, and outcome labels) or assurance effort (audits, calibration, checker maintenance, and approvals) rises. Reports should separate these costs and state the trigger, who has authority to respond, and how quickly the supervisor can act. The broader survey of recursive self-improvement by Chen et al. (2026d) likewise treats the closing of the loop as a continuum and identifies evaluation and the setting of research direction as persistent human bottlenecks. For physical systems, a meaningful claim about autonomy names which decisions became reliably automated and where in the loop supervision moved.

Proposing a change, admitting it, executing it, and judging its outcome can be run by the same people, as long as the four steps do not all depend on the same mutable component or unprotected signal and each stage retains evidence or control capable of exposing and containing failure in the preceding one.

## 4.3.5 Revalidating Safety Across Generations

Admission and rollback act on one candidate at a time; a system that revises its own learning procedure must also ask whether the evidence behind its safeguards survives the revision. Three questions recur. First, does earlier safety evidence still apply? A guard validated for one controller, action normalization, or reset routine holds only for what it was validated on. FAN shows that updating action-normalization statistics shifts the coordinates shared by training and execution, so a bound stated in the old coordinates would need rechecking, and Table 15 lists how other revisions can invalidate safety assumptions. Each generation therefore needs a record of which checks were run against which component versions, and a rule that marks dependent checks as stale when those versions change.

Second, who may change the safety boundary? ENPIRE builds its safety, reset, and verification services in a human-guided stage and exposes them as interfaces that its coding agents cannot modify, and RegenHarness forbids its updater to weaken its own acceptance rule; the same separation should extend to the improver, so that a revision of the learning procedure cannot also relax the checks that judge it.

Third, how are regressions across versions and bodies caught? A net gain can hide cases that the previous version solved, and a hardware change such as a replaced gripper can invalidate behavior that no software rollback restores; regression suites carried across generations and rerun after hardware changes keep earlier competence and safety margins visible. These requirements are inferred from the reviewed failures; testing them over several generations of a self-revising physical system is part of the open problem of surviving system changes (Section 4.2).

Beyond safety.

Self-improving robots also raise questions that safety engineering does not settle. Fleet learning gathers experience in homes and workplaces, as when OpenBot-Fleet (Müller et al., 2024) uploads data from user-operated robots, so consent, retention, and access to recorded scenes become part of the design of the learning loop. When a robot revises its own learning procedure, responsibility for a harmful update must also stay traceable; the provenance records and separated authority described above serve accountability as well as safety.

Synthesis

Keep the ability to learn safe, not only the current behavior

• 

Safety spans the whole loop. Exploration, resets, candidate testing, and execution after an update each create their own risk; securing the deployed policy covers only the last of them.

• 

Protect the evidence that judges change. One saved verifier in SEAR was wrong on 91% of the later rollouts it passed; a revisable evaluator needs a success evaluation that the candidate cannot rewrite.

• 

Separate authority and plan for irreversibility. No mutable component should both propose and judge a change, and because rollback restores only computational state, trial limits must precede admission.

• 

Safeguards that keep pace with self-modification. The reviewed gates, shields, and monitors are each validated in narrow settings, and ENPIRE’s safety and verification services are built once and then held fixed; the next step revalidates such safeguards across generations of a robot that revises its own learning procedure.

## 4.4 Making Improvement Recursive

A recursive improver must decide what to repair and what to try before it can learn from its repairs. This subsection takes these capabilities in turn, from diagnosis and experiment choice to transferable repair knowledge, and then designs the test that would show the loop closing.

Finding the bottleneck.

The agenda begins with diagnosis because changing the wrong component consumes physical trials and can obscure the evidence needed for the next attempt. The same observed failure can have several causes, as the missed grasp of Section 1 shows, so a failure label alone cannot determine which repair to choose. Calling a component the bottleneck therefore states a hypothesis to be tested against other, interacting explanations; logs can narrow those explanations but rarely determine which change will improve downstream behavior, and Table 16 separates evidence that supports a diagnosis before intervention from comparisons that test a candidate repair after intervention.

Table 16: Checks before repair and comparisons after intervention.

Hardware changes can still break it

Physical traces make this inference harder because they are partial and often censored by safety mechanisms. An intervention may prevent the failure that would have revealed the weakness, while the final rescued success may conceal which autonomous transition required correction. A diagnostic trace should align observations, proposed actions, executed actions, any shield or human modification, feedback versions, and intervention timing. Unobserved signals should stay marked as unknown, never counted as evidence that a component worked. The bottleneck is the component hypothesized to limit the next improvement, given the available repairs; diagnosing it does not presuppose a single independent root cause.

Failure explanation is a natural starting point. REFLECT (Liu et al., 2023c) summarizes multisensory robot experience hierarchically and asks a language model to explain failures and plan corrections, and AHA (Duan et al., 2025) fine-tunes a vision-language model on procedurally perturbed demonstrations to detect and explain manipulation failures. Both explain a failure from the recorded episode alone. A diagnostic system should go further, keeping competing explanations ranked and choosing a small, safe probe expected to separate them before attempting a costly repair. A concrete benchmark could inject matched policy, model, evaluator, and interface faults that produce similar terminal outcomes within the same task family. Single faults would test basic identification; unseen combinations and intervention-censored traces would test whether the method preserves uncertainty when causes interact or evidence is missing. Fault injection supplies controlled causal labels but not full realism, so the resulting repair choices should also be tested on held-out, naturally occurring hardware failures. Evaluation should measure calibrated cause rankings, information gained per probe, and, most importantly, whether the selected repair improves downstream behavior from matched starting points and physical budgets. This last test separates useful diagnosis from classification that merely names the injected fault.

Choosing the next experiment.

While several causes remain plausible, the most diagnostic experiment and the most useful learning experience can differ. Suppose a grasp failure could reflect either a missing recovery action or an incorrect contact model. Repeating the contact with additional force sensing may separate those explanations, but evidence of a contact-model error helps only if a model update can represent the discrepancy and a later learner can use the revised prediction. An expert correction supplies an immediately usable action target, yet may leave the contact-model error unresolved. An experiment is useful for learning only if an available update method can use the evidence it provides, so its value depends on which uncertainty it reduces and whether the resulting update improves behavior.

An experiment also incurs reset, wear, intervention, and safety costs immediately, whereas its learning benefit appears only after updating and redeployment. Some informative states cannot be revisited safely, yet a promising repair may need one. A staged selector could first use stored traces, counterfactual replay, or short model-based probes, then request new contact or correction only while the remaining explanations would lead to different usable updates. Cheap probes narrow the question but cannot resolve a physical distinction that their data or model omits.

A benchmark for this problem should compare joint experiment–update selection with fixed collection, information-gain-only selection, and a sequential baseline that diagnoses first and chooses an update afterward, scoring held-out behavioral improvement rather than predicted information gain under the reporting rules of Section 4.1. Ablating either the predicted information gain or the predicted usability of the evidence would show which part of the joint decision matters.

Transferring repair knowledge.

Recursion requires retaining what was learned from a repair, not only the repaired policy. A stored edit without its applicability conditions supports patch reuse, and a searcher that keeps rediscovering edits repeats a search rather than learning from earlier repairs.

Learning such knowledge requires histories that expose both success and failure. The same slip penalty can help one grasping policy and harm another because their exploration differs; a code repair can depend on an API, timing convention, sensor, or control rate. Each record should connect the proposed explanation and unresolved alternatives, attempted change, starting competence, component and interface versions, data and feedback, physical conditions, resources spent, and downstream successes or regressions. Failed revisions do more than serve as negative examples: they identify missing prerequisites or incorrect causal explanations. Selectively recording only accepted patches would make an editor appear more reliable while removing the evidence needed to decide when not to reuse them.

The main open problem is how to use accumulating real-robot revision histories to learn a procedure improver that transfers across failure types, and to verify that it also helps produce its own next improvement. If what is learned does not transfer beyond near-duplicates of past failures, repair knowledge amounts to a library of patches, and the two-round test below reduces to a test of reuse. The improver must separate transferable gains from those due to extra data, a stronger starting policy, or a changed apparatus, and recognize when to reuse an earlier repair, adapt it, or seek a different explanation. Benchmark tasks should vary starting competence, feedback versions, interfaces, and physical conditions while keeping tools, opportunities, and budgets matched. Direct retrieval of an old patch should be reported as reuse; a transferable repair principle should pass both comparisons of Figure 9 on held-out failure families.

Testing recursive improvement.

Can a system learn from attempted repairs to improve the way it produces further improvements? The two links of Section 2.2 describe a causal dependence across revisions, which no count of mutable components, checkpoints, or autonomous steps can establish. Section 4.1 sets out the judgments that a recursive claim must keep apart; the two-round test below would isolate either link for a learning procedure.

Where recursion could start.

Each of the four routes can supply the object of a recursive test: a rule for changing that route, together with records of how earlier changes fared.

For policies, the natural rule to revise is the one that chooses which experience to collect. A criticality model already steers collection toward likely failures and, in two simulated multi-round runs, is retrained on the current policy’s rollouts each round; a recursive version would also learn, from which collections taught later learners, how to revise that criterion.

For world models, the rule decides when a model may judge. A learned model is the closest physical counterpart of the executable tests that make digital RSI cheap, because it can screen candidate changes before hardware trials, but its judgments must themselves be checked. On a physical arm, a world-model judge fine-tuned only on successes scores most held-out failing sequences as success-like, and fine-tuning on execution-checked counterfactual failures reduces this sharply (Xu et al., 2026c). A recursive version would learn from such mismatches how to refresh the judge, so that each refresh leaves fewer errors for the next.

For external knowledge, the rule decides what to keep from a repair. Memories, skills, and code can store repair knowledge in inspectable form, with the applicability conditions that the preceding paragraphs ask each record to carry, and a frozen memory snapshot directly supplies the control described below.

For the learning procedure, the rule is the improver itself, such as a reward editor or the procedure that trains a skill-library editor. The test below starts there, because Eureka and PRACTICE already supply fixed-improver baselines. Across the four routes, what differs is where repair knowledge lives and how it can be frozen, not what the test must show.

How to test recursion.

A bounded test can focus on reward construction, as in the missed-grasp example of Section 3.3, or on skill-library editing (Figure 9), for which existing systems already supply baselines. Eureka is a reward improver whose knowledge does not change, and PRACTICE is a learned skill editor whose own improver, the staged procedure that trains it, is fixed. The proposed test extends the matched downstream comparison of Figure 8 from a revised procedure to the improver that produces it by comparing a learned improver before and after it learns from earlier repairs:

Figure 9: A proposed test of the two links of recursive self-improvement (R1 and R2). In R2, the updated improver revises itself with frozen or acquired knowledge, producing the control or next improver, respectively. Each then revises a learning procedure, which is tested through fresh matched learners under a common, protected outcome check; the next improver must beat both the control and the updated improver; for the updated improver, its learner from R1 serves as the baseline. The R2 control assumes that the acquired knowledge can be withheld without changing the parent improver, as with a memory snapshot; the count of four hardware-trained learners per seed assumes that one candidate per improver survives screening.

• 

Hold the new problem common. Give both versions the same new failure histories, tools, objectives, and revision budget. Let them generate or select candidate learning procedures, rather than only task controllers.

• 

Test what those procedures teach. Apply the candidates to matched learners and evaluate common physical outcomes. Report the cost of updating the improver separately.

• 

Follow the improver’s next revision. Given common new repair histories, let the updated improver propose a revision of itself and compare the result both with the unrevised improver and with a control revision of the same improver proposed with its earlier knowledge frozen. Repeating the first two tests on the resulting improvers links further gains to what was learned.

The first two steps test the first link and the third tests the second, as stated by the R1 and R2 inequalities of Section 2.2 (Figure 9); retention, performance under changed conditions, and sustainable resource use decide whether the relation is useful beyond a local controlled test. SEAR (Wang et al., 2026h) already supplies benchmark controls for such tests, separating retained experience from repeated attempts, task familiarity, privileged state, larger budgets, and chance.

What the test must control.

A successor can score higher because of accumulated experience or a stronger starting system rather than a better improvement mechanism, as a concurrent survey also notes (Zhou et al., 2026a). Three details decide whether the test is informative. First, freezing must match the form of the improver’s knowledge: a parametric improver is compared with its earlier checkpoint, a retrieval-based improver with its earlier memory snapshot, and an improver whose knowledge lives in code or prompts with their earlier versions, while the base model, tools, and budget stay the same. This suffices for R1, but R2 also requires the control revision to start from the same parent. That holds when the acquired knowledge can be withheld without changing the rest of the improver, as with a memory snapshot or separable code or prompts; when the knowledge lives in the improver’s weights, restoring the earlier checkpoint also changes the parent, so the R2 control for a parametric improver needs a design of its own. Second, the new failure families must be held out from everything the improver has learned from; otherwise the comparison measures memorization. Third, the physical cost should be staged: candidate procedures can be screened in simulation or on stored traces, so that hardware trials go to matched learners for the few candidates that survive. With one survivor per improver, the two comparisons need four hardware-trained learners per seed, because the updated improver’s learner from the first comparison also serves as the parent baseline of the second: about 1,200 refinement rollouts at FIERCE’s per-condition budget, excluding evaluation, resets, and repetition across failure families. Digital precedents run close analogues of both comparisons when evaluation is cheap; the physical version adds protected outcomes and a matched downstream learner. SpecRSI’s same-artifact comparison, which runs the original and the updated improver on one released artifact, is a digital instance of this control.

Three near-term programs follow from the agenda. First, combine cross-component diagnosis with joint experiment–update selection under matched multi-resource budgets, so that the correct repair and the cost of identifying it both determine success. Second, test versioned evaluators and repair knowledge on held-out failures and declared hardware or software changes, using protected outcome checks to separate transfer from drift. Third, connect these tests across at least two causally isolated procedure-revision rounds and report their full acquisition, maintenance, and revalidation costs.

Synthesis

The recursive target is knowledge about improving the learning procedure

• 

Test procedures, not controllers. Both versions of the improver must produce learning procedures, judged through matched learners.

• 

Freeze the knowledge, not the resources. The control keeps model, tools, and budget and withholds only the acquired repair knowledge, on held-out failure families.

• 

Where to start. Reward construction and skill-library editing yield inspectable revisions that simulation can screen, leaving four hardware-trained learners per seed when one candidate per improver survives.

\physicalaisectionaccent

PAIInk

## 5 Conclusion

This survey examined how robots that improve from physical experience can move toward recursive self-improvement, in which a system improves not only its behavior but also the process by which it improves. We positioned RSI within robot learning: reinforcement learning, self-training, continual learning, meta-learning, and AutoML say how updates are computed or what they must cope with, whereas a ladder of claims states what an experiment shows to have improved. Along that ladder, we reviewed how physical experience improves policies, world models, and knowledge, the learning procedures that produce them, and the improver that revises those procedures. What limits a robot’s next improvement (RQ1) is rarely visible in a terminal outcome; it is a hypothesis about missing experience, supervision, optimization, action generation, interfaces, or retention, to be tested by intervention rather than inferred from data volume or component accuracy. The changes that address it (RQ2) are kept in policies, world models, external knowledge, or the learning procedure, and they build on one another (RQ3) only under four conditions: feedback addresses remaining learning needs, the receiving component can use the change, its value survives changes in tasks or hardware, and continued practice remains safe and affordable. Physical constraints (RQ4) act through all four: incomplete sensing limits what a signal can distinguish; embodiment interfaces limit what a recipient can execute; wear, calibration drift, and hardware changes drive distribution shift; and reset, safety, and cost bound continued practice.

Existing studies, concentrated in manipulation, support behavioral improvement, later-learning benefits, and procedure improvement. Some systems also change their improver’s knowledge or tools, but no physical study reviewed here has yet isolated a gain attributable to recursion, which requires a controlled comparison against a version of the improver whose acquired knowledge is frozen. These answers remain provisional, because the four conditions are overlapping diagnostic lenses rather than independently validated constructs and much of the evidence comes from recent preprints with few training runs or trials.

Digital agents already supply mechanisms for revising their own code and tools. Whether these mechanisms work with physical experience and under physical constraints depends on the open problems identified here: evaluation on tasks the revising system cannot tune against, with matched learners for claims beyond behavior; gains that keep accumulating as data stop teaching and bottlenecks move; practice that stays safe while the procedure governing it changes; and an improver that learns to find bottlenecks, choose experiments, and transfer repair knowledge. Physical recursive self-improvement will be demonstrated not when a robot changes many times, but when knowledge of why one repair worked causally improves how the next repair is made, and when that improved capability, applied to new evidence, helps improve itself again without losing the ability to keep learning. Even a robot that becomes measurably better at rewriting its own recipe for a single family of failures would give the first such evidence.

## Appendix A Extended Landscape of Physical Self-Improvement

This appendix extends the coverage of the mechanisms reviewed in the main text. Appendix B records selected numerical outcomes and their denominators, and Appendix C collects the terms used throughout.

Table 17 shows, stage by stage, what people and systems contribute to the reviewed loops. Table 18 identifies the support for cross-domain interpretations. Manipulation supplies detailed component and downstream comparisons; navigation, locomotion, flight, and laboratories contribute particular reuse, adaptation, and experiment-selection mechanisms. Table 19 then extends the system coverage with policy post-training, autonomous resets, fleets, dynamics adaptation, reusable memory, continual learning, and contact-aware feedback. It includes both system-generated and expert-provided experience and distinguishes training from evaluation settings.

Table 17: Human and system contributions at each stage of an improvement loop. Contributions can coexist.

Stage

Human contribution

System contribution

Experience

Demonstrations; resets

Rollouts; task or state selection

Supervision

Corrections; outcome labels

Predicted reward; progress estimates

Update construction

Training design; code edits

Parameter fitting; generated code edits

Selection

Checkpoint or candidate choice

Filtering; candidate ranking

Validation

Outcome audit; safety review

Instrumented test; contract check

Deployment

Approval; physical setup

Execution of an approved release or rollback
