## Appendix B Reported Outcomes and Experimental Context

Table 20 collects numerical details that are secondary to the mechanism comparisons in the main text. Percentages retain each paper’s denominator and metric where available. Approximate values are identified as such. The settings, supervision, and acquisition budgets differ across rows, so the table is not a performance ranking.

Table 20: Selected reported outcomes, baselines, and evaluation settings. Icons mark where each reported number was measured.

System

Reported comparison

Setting and interpretation

SOARZhou et al., 2025c

 Mean success 28% $\to$ 57%; released dataset about 30.5K trajectories

Ten evaluation scenes across five tabletop settings. The updated arXiv text (its Section 4 and Figure 5; the dataset size also appears in its abstract and Appendix E) reports these values. The PMLR landing-page abstract instead states 25K trajectories.

SI-EFMGhasemipour et al., 2025

 LanguageTable approximately 62–63% $\to$ 87–88%; BananaTable approximately 63% $\to$ 85%

Approximate plotted rates in the real-robot experiments. BananaTable practice uses about eight hours on two robot stations. The learned feedback checkpoint stays fixed during policy improvement.

HIL-SERLLuo et al., 2025

 RAM insertion: 100% versus 29% for HG-DAgger; cycle time 4.8 versus 8.3 seconds

100 trials per condition. Matched imitation comparison in the main results table. Intervention, reward classification, and compliant control jointly define the learning system.

RECAPPhysical Intelligence et al., 2026

 More than $2\times$ throughput on diverse laundry and espresso; roughly half the failure rate

Relative to supervised specialization of the same pretrained policy.

RAPolicyYang et al., 2026a

 Joint-task success 52% $\to$ 88%

One joint five-task setting, alongside four single-task settings. Online training uses one to two hours per setting and includes human interventions.

SARM2/SPIRALChen et al., 2026e

 Crumpled-shorts folding: 4/12 $\to$ 8/12 with stage-aware reward versus 4/12 $\to$ 2/12 with sparse reward

Their Table 2; real bimanual arms. Both loops start from the same dense-reward checkpoint and run three rounds; rollout episodes differ, as expected for on-policy loops.

GR-RLLi et al., 2025b

 Shoe lacing: 45.7% $\to$ 83.3%, against behavior cloning on all demonstrations

Bimanual mobile robot. An offline critic used as a progress estimator filters demonstrations, mirror augmentation is added, and a latent-noise predictor is then trained online.

VLA-PrecisionSu et al., 2026

 98.3% mean success over 180 trials on nine chemistry tasks, against 67.8% for a $\pi_{0.5}$ baseline

The critic ensemble is calibrated from real experience; the baseline is trained with more demonstrations.

VERITASZhang and Shah, 2026

 SIMPLER Stack Blocks: 31.3% $\to$ 59.2% after fine-tuning

656 verified successful rollouts across four simulated tasks are used for fine-tuning. Separate real-robot comparisons at matched data sizes test verifier-curated data against human teleoperation.

SILVRLuo et al., 2026a

 Meta-World unseen tasks: 26.8% with the inverse-dynamics translator frozen versus 44.2% when it is also fine-tuned

Their Table A11: twelve unseen tasks, three seeds, fourth iteration; the video planner is fine-tuned in both conditions. The hardware setup keeps the translator fixed.

RISEYang et al., 2026b

 Brick sorting, backpack packing, box closing: 85%, 85%, 95% versus 50%, 40%, 60%

Twenty hardware trials per task. The baseline is the authors’ matched $\pi_{0.5}$ RECAP reimplementation.

PlayWorldYin et al., 2026b

 Fold the Towel: 10% $\to$ 75% at the best checkpoint

Twenty hardware trials per checkpoint; two tasks overall. The 65-point absolute gain is followed by regression with further model-based training. Thirty hours of autonomous play precede the model/policy stages.

World-GymnastSharma et al., 2026

 Hardware: above SIMPLER-trained RL on three of four WidowX tasks (e.g., 72% vs 32%)

The VLA is trained in a learned video model with GPT-4o rewards; SIMPLER remains better on closing a drawer.

DexTouch-WMQin et al., 2026

 FTP-1 mean score: 0.73 all-real versus 0.69 with half generated by the human–robot model

Their Table IV; four hardware tasks; means rounded to two decimals. Compare 200 real trajectories with 100 real plus 100 generated. Partial-credit scores. Data from the human–robot model lower all three policy-family means; data from the robot-only-adapted model give mixed results.

PLDXiao et al., 2026a

 Cube pickup: 30/30 for PLD data, 16/30 for RLPD data, 10/30 for human demonstrations

Two real-task distillation tests. Peg insertion is 30/30 for all three data conditions. PLD and RLPD each supply 200 successful episodes and all three datasets fine-tune $\pi_{0}$ ; the human-data size is not stated, and source collector acquisition differs.

Robo-CortexChan et al., 2026

 SPL 24.03 $\to$ 39.33 with transferred heuristics; difference 15.30

Heuristic-transfer ablation on Habitat’s unseen image-goal split (their Table 3), without online updates, against Basic Pipeline. SPL is success weighted by path length. Hardware evidence is separate and preliminary: two indoor scenes with ten navigation goals.

VASOYang et al., 2026e

Formal compliance 97.2% with handcrafted alignment, 96.8% with automatic alignment; fewer than 100 optimization samples

Aggregate main table over generated plans; Jackal and PX4 executions are shown separately. The percentages are formal-specification scores, not measured probabilities of physical safety; the baseline task-completion comparison is evaluated in simulation.

SHAPERWang et al., 2026e

 VLABench success 34.50% versus 28.25% for the seed agent

Four held-out simulation splits. Skill-only and harness-only controls test interacting components; the headline comparison changes both.

ZettaDing et al., 2026

 LIBERO-PRO macro-average 32.00% $\to$ 71.13%; RoboCasa 73.56% $\to$ 93.56%

Forty LIBERO-PRO task-setting pairs and eighteen RoboCasa tasks. RoboCasa spans four repair rounds. The abstract’s 90.8% LIBERO-PRO headline uses a different aggregation.

RoboHarn-EvoBao et al., 2026

 Physical feedback resolves more than 83% of erroneous memory entries while retaining 95.8% of valid ones

Entries are task- and action-level knowledge with stated applicability; zero-shot transfer of simulation-acquired knowledge is reported in Section 3.1.3.

Code transferKuroki and Tang, 2026

 Nine-task mean: 45.2% without references, 41.5% with initial code, 57.0% with optimized code

Their Table III: three independent generation runs, ten tests per task/run, 270 tests per condition in total. Same coding model and limits; privileged simulated observations and realized compute remain relevant.

RATsZhang et al., 2026f

 Held-out LIBERO-PRO success +20.6 percentage points over the CaP-Agent0 baseline

A code-skill library distilled from successful play.

RoboSkillXie et al., 2026c

 Hard tasks: 80.0% $\to$ 88.3%

Real-robot evaluation of a reusable skill library.

GLIDESong et al., 2026c

 Teleoperated collection successes: 0, 1, and 0 of 10 without guardrails versus 7, 9, and 9 with refined guardrails

Their Table 1; three real tasks, ten trials each. Unrefined guardrails already reach 6 and 7 on two tasks.

SEESLi et al., 2026l

 GR00T N1.5: 47.2% $\to$ 55.7% on RoboCasa365 atomic tasks and 10.2% $\to$ 13.1% on seen composite tasks

Three rounds. States before the most frequently stalling sub-skill are restored in simulation, and that skill family is trained with PPO against an LLM-written success check.

REVOLVELiu et al., 2026b

 Judgment accuracy 72.0% $\to$ 80.5%; policy success 40.0% $\to$ 58.5%

Four real tasks across the reported history. Memory and policy co-evolve, supported by a task-specific reset policy and a classical correction controller configured before the loop; the two trends do not isolate a causal memory contribution, which the component test of Section 13 would.

Read alongside the mechanism tables, these results distinguish isolated component effects from pipeline gains, hardware outcomes from simulated or formal scores, and acquisition from maintenance costs. Section 4.1 discusses the corresponding comparison and reporting requirements.

## Appendix C Glossary

This glossary summarizes the terminology used in the review; section references point to the relevant discussion.

Table 21: Terms used in this review.

Term

Meaning

Section

Action chunk

A short sequence of future actions that a policy outputs at once.

2.1

Admission gate

A rule that turns protected evidence into a decision on whether a candidate update may run.

4.3.3

Attempt, retry, round

An attempt is one try at a task; a retry is a further attempt on the same task instance within one episode; a learning round ends with an update from which later episodes start.

2.2

Behavioral improvement

The revised system executes better under a common task test.

2.2

Bottleneck

The component hypothesized to limit the next improvement, given the available repairs.

4.4

Continued practice

Whether another informative trial remains safe and affordable.

2.2

Cost accounting

An account that separates setup and signal construction, acquisition of the revision, downstream training and evaluation, deployment, and maintenance.

4.1

Evaluator

A component that supplies outcome judgments for learning: a person, a hand-written check, or a learned reward model such as a success detector.

2.1

Improver

Whatever generates or selects revisions of the learning procedure: a person, a fixed rule, a search process, or a learned component.

2.2

Informative feedback

Whether experience distinguishes the remaining learning need.

2.2

Later-learning benefit

An acquired artifact (data, weights, a model, a skill, or code) helps a subsequent learner.

2.2

Learner

The component that acquires task capabilities.

2.2

Learning procedure

The decisions that turn experience into updates: what to collect, how to judge it, and how to construct the update.

2.2

Level of support (R1, R2)

Mechanism only (machinery through which the improver could change), uncontrolled result (a comparison consistent with a link but without a control that withholds only the acquired knowledge), or controlled result (a frozen-knowledge or fixed-improver control with matched learners and budgets).

2.2

Matched learner

A learner that differs from its comparison only in the component under test and shares its starting conditions, resources, and evaluation protocol, so that two inputs or procedures can be compared by what they teach.

2.2

Payback period

The period within which the trials a reusable tool saves, minus its maintenance, must exceed what it cost to build.

4.2

Procedure improvement

A revised collector, evaluator, editor, or updater teaches a matched learner better.

2.2

Protected success evaluation

A success evaluation that a candidate update cannot rewrite and that remains informative about the relevant physical failure.

4.3.2

Record of assumptions

A record, for each candidate change, of what changed, which safeguard depends on it, what was rechecked, and which hazards remain.

4.3.1

Recursive self-improvement

Repair knowledge improves the improver (R1), and the improver, once improved, helps produce its own next improvement (R2).

2.2

Repair

An update made in response to an observed failure or limitation.

2.2

Repair knowledge

Knowledge retained from earlier repairs: which failure distinction motivated a change, under what conditions it applied, and what it improved downstream.

2.2

Retained change

What experience leaves behind for later use: a policy, a model, a memory, a skill, software, or a change to the learning procedure.

2.2

Robustness to distribution shift

Whether a change remains useful after the policy, model, task, or hardware changes, including changes that the improvement itself caused.

2.2

Success evaluation

The person, sensor, or model that decides whether an attempt succeeded.

2.4

Support (of a model)

The range of actions and contacts for which a model has comparable training examples.

4.2

Updater

A procedure that maps the learner’s state, experience, and objective to an operation such as a parameter step, a loss function, a skill edit, or a code patch.

3.2.4

Usability

Whether the component that must consume a change can represent, optimize, and execute it.

2.2

Uses of retained change

Behavior; prediction and practice; reuse and recovery; and the learning procedure. Each names how a change is used next.

2.2

Validity conditions

A statement attached to a retained change: its required inputs, action meanings, physical envelope, software dependencies, and the evidence under which it last worked.

4.2

## Appendix D Gallery

The figures in this appendix collect images released with several of the reviewed systems. They show the settings behind the results discussed in Section 3 and Appendix B: robots practising on hardware, fleets that pool experience, simulators and world models paired with real setups, agents that write the code that drives a robot, laboratories that run experiments on their own, and a wider range of robot bodies and settings, from legged and aerial robots to arms working in homes and outdoors. Each caption cites the work and names the source of the image.
